<summary>This title page introduces a 1755 compendium of chemical and artisanal "secrets," offering instructions for making pigments, varnishes, imitation gemstones, and metal alloys. It is addressed to a wide range of craftsmen and "enthusiasts," reflecting the Enlightenment-era interest in practical science and the democratization of trade knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page is the beginning of the preface, following the title page. The catchword "möch" indicates that the next page will likely begin with "möchte" (might/would), continuing the final sentence.</meta>

# Preface.

## Most Esteemed Reader!

In this treatise, I am fulfilling the promise made on my title page and, with all faithfulness, I deliver several <term>practical experiments<note>original German: "Kunst-Stücke." In this era, the term referred to skillful demonstrations, chemical recipes, or mechanical "tricks" that proved a scientific or artistic principle.</note></term> that have been proven reliable through frequently repeated trials. I do this so that it cannot be said of this <term>treatise<note>original: "Tractat"</note></term>—at least not with any basis in truth—as is so often said of other books: that the title page promises much, but within the work itself, that which was promised is neither found nor carried out. No person who grants this writing even a small measure of attention will be able to claim that it contains any falsehood. Indeed, to ensure the absolute certainty of the subjects discussed here, I have kept them secret for many years, so as not to place before the art-loving reader anything that might—

<summary>The author introduces his treatise by asserting its practical reliability, claiming that his work actually delivers on the promises made on the title page. He emphasizes that the experiments included have been tested for years to ensure their truthfulness before being made public.</summary>
<keywords>preface, credibility, experimental proof, practical arts, secret knowledge, Early Modern science</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from the previous page. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the author's thought regarding the reliability of his experiments. The catchword from the previous page was "möch," which here becomes "möchte" (might).</meta>

might result in anything but a certain and desired outcome for everyone who performs a trial. It may indeed be that some readers encounter things here that are already known to them; however, I am confident that these are in the minority. Far more readers will find the subjects discussed in this <term>treatise<note>original German: "Tractate"</note></term> entirely new—individuals who have often striven in vain to acquire such knowledge and for whom this sincere instruction and guidance will be a source of pleasure. 

**The dissolving of copal resin for lacquering**<note>Copal is a hard, amber-like resin that was notoriously difficult to dissolve in solvents. Mastering a clear copal varnish was a high achievement in 18th-century finishing.</note> has, until now, remained a great secret to most and has been sought after with genuine longing. This process is demonstrated very clearly here, and the method for applying the finest and most durable lacquer to metal and wood is thoroughly explained.

**Fire gilding**<note>A process where a gold-mercury amalgam is applied to metal and then heated. The mercury evaporates—often creating toxic fumes for the artisan—leaving a permanent layer of gold.</note>, which <term>sword-cutlers<note>original: "Schwerdtfeger." These were specialized artisans who finished, polished, and decorated sword blades and hilts.</note></term> and all who possess the knowledge keep strictly secret, is revealed with complete honesty in this little treatise. The disclosure of this art alone is worthy of great esteem. The

<summary>In this portion of the preface, the author asserts that his instructions are reliable and will succeed for any reader who tries them. He highlights two major "secrets" he is revealing: the method for dissolving copal resin for high-quality lacquer and the guarded technique of fire gilding used by sword-cutlers.</summary>
<keywords>copal resin, lacquering, fire gilding, sword-cutlers, craft secrets, 18th-century technology, varnishing</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. The previous page ended with "The," and this page picks up the sentence by addressing the "most esteemed reader." ## Preface.

most esteemed reader will also find revealed here the secret of making the true, genuine, and finest **carmine** <note>A vivid red pigment historically derived from the cochineal insect.</note>; to say nothing of **ultramarine** <note>An extremely expensive blue pigment made from ground lapis lazuli; at the time, it was often more valuable than gold.</note>, which is described here in a way that allows it to be brought to a very fine state. The clearest concepts and trials for making the most beautiful glazes are given, from which thorough description potters and other artists can promise themselves much pleasure and great benefit. The **composition of stones** <note>This refers to the creation of "paste" or "strass"—high-quality lead glass used to simulate diamonds and other colored gemstones.</note> is described in the clearest detail, including all the specific circumstances and necessary manual techniques. Everyone knows how many have acquired a considerable fortune through this science in **Bohemia** <note>A region in the modern-day Czech Republic that was the historical center of the European glass and decorative stone industry.</note> and other kingdoms and places. In some cities, there are as many as 300 **gem-cutters** <note>original: "Steinschneider." Artisans skilled in the lapidary arts of cutting and polishing precious and semi-precious stones.</note>, who have made this art of cutting and polishing stones wealthy and famous throughout the whole world. I flatter myself that I am performing a pleasant service for my most esteemed readers with these and other useful **experiments** detailed in this <term>treatise<note>original German: "Tractate"</note></term>, and I promise that if these, for now clearly

<summary>In this section of the preface, the author promotes his book's instructions for creating premium pigments like carmine and ultramarine, as well as ceramic glazes. He specifically highlights the lucrative art of creating and cutting imitation gemstones, citing the success of the industry in Bohemia.</summary>
<keywords>carmine, ultramarine, glazes, pottery, artificial gemstones, Bohemia, gem-cutting, lapidary arts, chemical experiments</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page completes the author's preface, continuing the sentence from the previous page where the author expressed hope for his work's reception. The text transitions from the justification of the book to the author's pledge of transparency.</meta>

# Preface.

described **artistic secrets** <note>original German: "Kunst-Stücke." This term refers to specialized recipes, chemical processes, or technical demonstrations that were often kept as trade secrets.</note> might acquire some approval among the art-loving world, I intend to spare neither cost nor effort in the future to find more, and to serve **the curious** <note>original Latin: "Curiosis." In the 18th century, "the curious" referred to collectors, amateur scientists, and intellectually inquisitive people who sought out new knowledge and "wonders."</note> with them. I therefore proceed to the fulfillment of my promise; in the explanation and instruction of the **subject matters** <note>original Latin: "Materien." Here referring to the specific materials and chemical topics to be discussed.</note> undertaken for execution, I will withhold nothing, serve my most honored readers with all faithfulness, and recommend myself to you most highly,

->Freystadt, 1755.<-

The Author.

<keywords>Enlightenment, transparency, artistic secrets, Freystadt, technical recipes, the curious</keywords>

->1.<-

### To prepare the finest Carmine, so that it obtains the most sublime and beautiful purple color.

<image-desc>An ornamental drop-cap 'D' set within a square woodcut frame decorated with intricate floral vines and foliage.</image-desc>
The principal reason why the red carmine pigment <note>original: "Carmin-Farbe." Carmine is a deep red pigment traditionally derived from the cochineal insect.</note> has hitherto been so dearly paid for, and valued almost as highly as gold, is likely because so very few know how to make it. It may also be, however, that it is so precious because much skill <note>original: "Kunst." In this historical context, this refers to practical artistry or craftsmanship.</note> and specific knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft." Referring here to specialized technical or systematic understanding.</note> are required for it, and not everyone can imitate it; since an oversight that appears very slight at the beginning often brings the greatest harm in the end. For if one wishes to make the same and lets it boil just a little too long, a very dark color results from it; or, if for its extraction <term>extraction</term> <note>The chemical process of drawing out the color from the raw material using a liquid solvent.</note> a hard water mixed with salts <note>original: "Salien," an archaic term for various mineral salts.</note>—

## I.

# To prepare the finest carmine, so that it receives the highest and most beautiful purple color.

<image-desc>A decorative drop-cap initial 'D' set within an ornate woodcut frame of leaves and flowers.</image-desc>

The primary reason why red carmine pigment <note>Carmine is a bright red pigment traditionally derived from the crushed bodies of the cochineal insect. In the early modern period, it was one of the most expensive and sought-after pigments for both painting and dyeing.</note> has been paid for so dearly until now, and valued almost equal to gold, is likely because very few people know how to make it. However, it may also be that it is so precious because much skill and a particular science <note>original: "Wissenschaft." In this period, "science" referred to specialized, systematic knowledge or a technical craft.</note> is required for it, and not everyone can imitate it; since a mistake that seems very minor at the start often results in the greatest damage at the end. For if one wishes to make it and lets it boil just a little too long, it becomes a very dark color; or, if for its extraction <term>extraction</term> <note>The process of using a solvent (like water or alcohol) to draw out the color or essential components from a raw material—in this case, the cochineal insect.</note> a hard water mixed with salts—

**Vocabulary:** **Carmine, purple color, extraction, salts, skill/art, science/knowledge**

<summary>This page introduces a guide on preparing high-quality carmine pigment, explaining that its high cost is due to both the secrecy of the process and the precise technical skill required to avoid ruining the color during production.</summary>
<keywords>carmine, pigment, dye-making, purple, cochineal, chemical extraction, 18th-century chemistry</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preparing carmine pigment, following the warning on the previous page about how the quality of water and boiling time affect the final color.</meta>

mountain water is used, it falls even into a violet color; Or, if it is not well covered during drying, in warm air and solar heat in a pure and clean room where there is no dust at all, or if it is prepared by the warm heat of a stove, it likewise turns out too dark. All this has happened to me personally; because it is nevertheless a color that costs a great deal of money, one must take very great care during its preparation and precisely note the following useful principles.

One puts 5 pounds of very clearly crushed <term>Cochineal</term> <note>The dried bodies of the *Dactylopius coccus* insect, used to produce brilliant red dyes and pigments.</note>, along with 1 <term>Loth</term> <note>A historical German unit of weight, approximately 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of powdered <term>Alum</term> <note>A chemical compound used here as a mordant to help the dye fix to a substrate or to help the pigment precipitate.</note>, into a small copper kettle with water and lets it boil; but, by the clock, for no longer than 7 minutes. Then, when the boiling is over, the clear color is strained through a clean cloth; the coarse cochineal, however, remains at the bottom and no longer belongs to the <term>Carmine</term>. Instead, one saves it to make a fine <term>Florentine Lake</term> <note>A "lake" is a pigment manufactured by precipitating a dye with a metallic mordant; Florentine Lake was a specific high-quality red variety.</note> from it, which I will also show later. Before this, however, one must have dissolved 1 pound of **English Tin** in <term>Aqua-Regis</term> <note>original: "Aqua-Regis," literally "Royal Water." A highly corrosive mixture of acids (nitric and hydrochloric) capable of dissolving noble metals and tin.</note>. This is prepared as follows: One puts into a strong and good <term>Aquafort</term> <note>original: "Aquafort," literally "Strong Water." Historically refers to nitric acid.</note>, which is otherwise used to dissolve silver, a little salt—for example, in 1 pound of Aquafort, 3 Loth of salt—and lets it dissolve what it will, then pours it off, and such an Aquafort is ready-

## To make the very finest
## Florentine Lake.
-> <-

This color is indeed also costly, and the pound is worth 8 **Reichsthaler** <note>A silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire; 8 thalers was a significant sum for a pound of pigment.</note>; however, it can be prepared most usefully and advantageously when one makes **carmine** <term>Carmin</term>; for, a large quantity of **cochineal** <note>A red dye made from the dried bodies of female scale insects found on cacti.</note> remains left over, from which this **Florentine Lake** <term>Florentiner Lack</term> is thereafter most easily made. It also pays for all the costs spent on the carmine, and the very best color of the carmine is the profit therein; if one only proceeds correctly with it and is located in a place where no saline, hard waters must be used for leaching, so that it becomes a beautiful ruby or deep purple in color.

Likewise, take **English tin** <term>englisch Zinn</term>, dissolve it in such a previously described **aqua-fortis** <term>Aquafort</term> or **parting-water** <term>Scheidewasser</term> <note>Both terms refer to nitric acid, used here to dissolve the metal.</note>, and give the **Florentine Lake** its **body** <term>Corpus</term> <note>The "body" is the solid base that allows a liquid dye to become a solid pigment (lake).</note> therewith—once the **residue** <term>Resitium</term> <note>original: "Resitium." A Latin-derived term for the material left over after the first extraction.</note> of the carmine has been well boiled again in fresh water; for this no longer harms the color at all. Thus, a beautiful, delicate, and somewhat brownish color falls to the bottom, which is the true **Florentine Lake**. This must likewise be leached, or washed out with pure warm water, and gently dried in the heat of the sun...

# 2.

## To make the very finest Florentine Lake.

This color is indeed also precious, and a pound is worth 8 imperial thalers <note>original: "Reichs-Thaler." A standard silver coin of the Holy Roman Empire; 8 thalers would have been a significant sum for a pigment.</note>; however, it can be prepared most usefully and advantageously when one makes **carmine**; for a large quantity of **cochineal** <note>A red dye made from crushed scale insects.</note> remains afterward, from which this **Florentine Lake** can then be made most easily. It also pays for all the costs spent on the carmine, and the very best color of the carmine is the profit therein; provided that one proceeds correctly and is in a location where no saline, hard waters must be used for leaching, so that it becomes a beautiful ruby or deep purple in color.

Take likewise **English tin**, dissolve <note>original: "solvire."</note> it in such an **aqua fortis** <term>Aquafort</term> or **parting-water** <term>Scheidewasser</term> <note>Both terms refer to nitric acid, used here to dissolve tin to create a mordant—a substance that fixes the dye.</note> as described before, and give the **Florentine Lake** its body <note>original: "Corpus." In early chemistry, this refers to the solid base or substrate that allows a liquid dye to precipitate into a solid pigment "lake."</note> with it (once the residue <note>original: "Residuum."</note> of the carmine has been boiled properly again in fresh water; for this no longer harms the color at all): thus a beautiful, delicate, and somewhat brownish color falls to the bottom, which is the true **Florentine Lake**. This must likewise be leached, or washed out with clean, warm water, and gently in the warmth of the sun

### 4.
## To make a minor and inferior Lake.

Because precious colors are not always used for painting, but rather minor colors are needed for many things, I will also present here a simpler and easier way to make such a color: Take red shavings, called **Pernambuco** <note>original: "Fernabock." This refers to Brazilwood, a common source of red dye in the early modern period.</note>, 1 pound; boil these in several measures of pure and soft river-water. To this, finally add half a pound of **kermes grains** <note>original: "Cachau- oder Cocels-Körner." Scale insects used for red dye.</note>, let these boil together for another half hour, then add 2 <term>lots</term> <note>A "Lot" was a pre-metric unit of weight in German-speaking lands, roughly equal to 15-17 grams or about half an ounce.</note> of **cochineal**, and let it simmer for another quarter of an hour; afterwards, pour it through a sieve and clean it of the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "Fecibus." The solid sediment or waste material remaining after the extraction.</note>. Pour this extraction onto finely ground chalk, make a dough and round balls from it, and then dry them over a gentle fire in the manner described previously.

### 5.
## To make Ultramarine.

**Ultramarine** is a beautiful blue color and, because of its laborious preparation, a quite expensive one,

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 6, finishing the description of Ultramarine blue. The previous page ended with "Ultramarine is a beautiful blue color and, because of its laborious preparation, a quite expensiv..."</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<sig>A 4</sig>

...color, very delicate, and almost the most precious that can ever be prepared on Earth; for it exceeds gold in value, if it is made right well. Strictly speaking, it is a mineral color, made from <term>Lapis Lazuli</term> <note>original: "Lapis Lazuli." A deep-blue metamorphic rock which was the only source of true ultramarine before the 19th century.</note>, a gemstone of an excellently high blue color. If this stone does not carry golden veins <note>These "golden veins" are actually inclusions of pyrite (fool's gold), often found in high-quality Lapis Lazuli.</note> with it, it is not quite so expensive; because not everyone knows how to use it and how to make this color from it. It is, however, prepared as follows:

Initially, one crushes this stone coarsely in a mortar, puts the crushed material into a melting crucible, places it in the fire to <term>calcine</term> <note>original: "Calciniren." The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove impurities or change its physical state.</note>, pours linseed oil on top into the crucible, and lets everything burn out; when it has glowed well for several hours, one puts it into wine-vinegar; thus the stone crumbles and detaches itself completely from the "wild" rocks or quartzes. Once this has happened, it must be placed in a levigation or sifting-trough, and the white sand and pebble-grains must be washed and levigated away from it; for these must all be washed out clean. Then one grinds it on a <term>porphyry stone</term> <note>original: "Prophyr-Stein." A very hard volcanic rock used by artists as a slab for grinding pigments into a fine powder.</note> to a very delicate powder, then grinds it with nut oil, and dries it.

If one wants this color to be even more beautiful and shining than this, one can prepare a <term>mixture</term> <note>original: "Mixtum." A Latin term for a medicinal or chemical mixture.</note> of fat and resin in the following manner: linseed oil, white wax, resin, Burgundy pitch, turpentine, colophony <note>original: "Calphonium." ### 4.
### To make a low-grade and ordinary Lake.

Because expensive colors are not always required for painting, but rather cheaper colors are used for many things, I will also present here a lesser and easier way to make such a color: Take 1 pound of red shavings, called **Brazilwood** <note>original: "Fernabock," referring to Pernambuco wood, a common source of red dye.</note>; boil these in several measures of pure and soft river-water. To this, finally add half a pound of **Coccus grains** <note>original: "Cachau- oder Cocels-Körner." As noted previously, these are likely kermes insects.</note>, and let these boil together for another half hour. Then add 2 <term>Loth</term> <note>A traditional German unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of **Cochineal**, and let it boil down for another quarter of an hour. Afterward, pour it through a sieve and clean it of the <term>dregs</term> <note>original Latin: "Fecibus," referring to the solid sediment or residue.</note>. Pour this extract onto finely ground chalk, make a dough and round balls from it, and then dry them over a gentle fire in the manner described above.

A concentrated liquid solution of potassium carbonate created by allowing the salt to absorb moisture from the air.</note>. Place it in a sealed glass, then into a baking oven when the bread is slid in; as soon as the bread is finished baking in the oven, the color is also ready.

### 7.

## To make Prussian Blue, or Berlin Blue.

This color does not originate from the mineral kingdom; rather, it is made partly from ox blood, and partly from ox or cow horns. Its proper name is Berlin Blue, because it was first invented in Berlin by Mr. von Diesbach <note>Heinrich Diesbach was a German color maker who accidentally discovered Prussian Blue around 1704 while trying to make a red pigment. It was the first stable and relatively lightfast synthetic blue.</note>. It is prepared in the following way:

Dry ox blood in a large pan in an oven, and when it is dry, crush it small again in a mortar; then take saltpeter <note>Potassium nitrate</note> and tartar <note>original: "Weinstein." Potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking.</note>, an equal amount of each, and deflagrate them together—that is, ignite and burn them with a glowing coal—so that half as much white salt remains as it weighed at first. With this crushed salt, mix 3 pounds of dried and crushed ox blood on an iron shovel made for this purpose; let this flow together and glow over a charcoal fire. This gives off a strong smell like <term>Spirit of Sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "Spiritus sal armoniaci." An old name for an aqueous solution of ammonia.</note>. Now you must have prepared a lye in which several pounds of alum have been dissolved in pure river water or other soft water; into this lye, pour the glowing blood and let it be quenched therein. Now pour the lye off from this residue and pour it into a clean pot,

## To make fine Verdigris.

### 7.

## To make Prussian Blue, or Berlin Blue.

This color does not take its origin from the mineral kingdom; rather, it is made partly from ox blood and partly from the horns of oxen or cows. Its proper name is Berlin Blue, because it was first invented in Berlin by Mr. von Diesbach <note>Heinrich Diesbach was a color maker who accidentally discovered Prussian Blue around 1704. Pour the glowing blood into this lye and let it be quenched therein. ### 8.
## To make fine Verdigris.

To make verdigris is a **secret art** <note>original: "Arcanum." In the early modern period, this referred to a specialized trade secret or a hidden alchemical mystery.</note>, the discovery of which has cost many people wasted effort. While many books give extensive instruction on its preparation, those who wish to learn to prepare it from such books must admit that they found their hopes deceived. It requires many **experiments** <note>original: "Experimenta."</note> if one wishes to obtain something substantial from this color and turn it into a profit. Montpellier was famous for verdigris production, utilizing the humidity and the remains of grapes from the regional wine industry.</note>

### II.
### How to Dissolve Copal Resin for
### Varnishing Wood.

Melt or liquefy the <term>copal resin</term> <note>original: "Gummi-Copal." A very hard, clear resin from tropical trees used to create high-quality, durable varnishes.</note> in a spoon over the fire and pour it out so that all impurities are removed and the copal becomes clear and pure. Afterward, melt <term>rosin</term> <note>original: "Calphonium," likely a variation of Colophonium. A resin obtained from pine trees.</note> in a spoon and likewise pour it off to remove the impurities. Next, take 1 part of this purified copal and 1 part of the rosin; melt these together in a spoon over the fire. In this manner, the rosin dissolves the copal, and this mixture can then be dissolved in any <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "Spiritus." A high-proof alcohol or similar solvent.</note>, and becomes <unclear>bright</unclear>...

<summary>This page concludes the preparation of gold pigment for glass painting by mixing it with borax and lavender oil, and begins a recipe for wood varnish made from copal and pine resins.</summary>
<keywords>gold calx, borax, crucible, spike oil, glass painting, copal resin, rosin, colophony, wood varnish, solvent</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page completes the instructions for purifying copal resin for wood varnish from the previous page before introducing a specialized technique for metal varnish. It continues the discussion on the difficulty of dissolving hard resins.</meta>

and clear for varnishing. This is a secret trick still unknown to many; for <term>copal</term> <note>original: "Copal." A hard, translucent tree resin prized for making durable, high-gloss varnishes.</note>, being the finest varnish, cannot initially be dissolved in <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Vini." An old term for high-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note> or other substances, which has always been a cause for complaint. However, if a <term>medium</term> <note>original: "Medium." An intermediate substance used here to bridge the chemical gap between the resin and the solvent.</note> is used so that one substance acts upon the other, the resinous body is thereby unlocked.

->12.<-

## To Dissolve Copal Resin for Varnishing Metal.

Crush the copal into small pieces, place them in a small new pot, and let them boil thoroughly in water until a white, milk-like liquid appears. Then pour this off and pour fresh, pure river water over it. (In the absence of soft river water, rainwater is good, as it is also very soft. Hard well water, in which soap does not lather, is not suitable for such work.) Now boil it again until the copal has been boiled into nothing but milk in the water. Collect these liquids together and drip <term>wine vinegar</term> <note>original: "Weinessig."</note> into it drop by drop; this will cause the copal resin to <term>precipitate</term> <note>original: "präcipitirt." To fall out of a solution as a solid powder.</note>. Afterward, boil it once more in wine vinegar so that it is properly purified; in this way, it will dissolve in any kind of alcohol.

<summary>This page explains a chemical "trick" for dissolving stubborn copal resin by first boiling it into a "milk" in soft water and then precipitating it with vinegar to make it soluble in alcohol.</summary>
<keywords>copal resin, varnish, spirit of wine, solvent, precipitation, metal varnishing, water quality</keywords>

## 13.
## To prepare Chinese Lacquer.

Take 1 measure of good <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Vini." High-proof alcohol used as a solvent.</note>, 10 <term>Loth</term> <note>original: "Loth." A historical German unit of weight, roughly equal to 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of the copal prepared as previously described, 8 Loth of <term>sandarac</term> <note>original: "Sandarach." A brittle, aromatic resin from cypress-like trees, used for hard, lustrous finishes.</note>, and 8 Loth of <term>gum-lac</term> <note>original: "Gummi-Lack." A resinous secretion, often called shellac in its refined form.</note>. Put these into a glass vessel and seal it tightly; let it stand for one or two days so that the <term>ingredients</term> <note>original: "Species." Referring here to the dry resins.</note> may soften. Then place the glass in a <term>bain-marie</term> <note>original: "Balneum Mariæ." Literally "Mary's Bath," a method of indirect heating where the vessel is placed in boiling water.</note>—that is, in a kettle of water—and boil it so that everything blends together well. Afterward, press it through a clean cloth, put it into a fresh glass, and let it cool there. In this manner, the lacquer can be prepared for varnishing wood and various other objects.

# 17.
## On Oil of Vitriol.

That <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleum-Vitrioli." Concentrated sulfuric acid, a powerful mineral acid used as a solvent.</note> is a dissolver of colors, and how it behaves toward colors as a mineral substance, can be seen in this modern age by the "new green" that is treated with such oil. This color is used to dye silk and wool fabrics and is now known almost throughout the whole world under the name of <term>Great Hayner's Green</term> <note>original: "grossen Hayners, Grün." Likely referring to "Saxon Green," a method of dissolving indigo in sulfuric acid to create a vibrant green dye, perfected in the town of Grossenhain.</note>. It is no longer a secret at all that this is made from...

...prepared blue <term>indigo</term> <note>original: "Indigo." A deep blue dye historically derived from plants of the genus *Indigofera*.</note>, which is dissolved and resolved in mineral <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleum-Vitrioli." Concentrated sulfuric acid.</note>, an art practiced nowadays in England, Holland, and in almost all parts of Germany. However, whether blue indigo is a composed vegetable color, or whether it is prepared from minerals and found as an "earth" within our Mother Earth, is as yet recognized by few. Therefore, it has often happened that many have dared to manufacture it from mere vegetable matter, for which the blue <term>bilberries</term> <note>original: "Heidelbeeren." Often refers to European blueberries; the author is mocking the attempt to make permanent dye from berry juice, which fades easily.</note> provided the occasion for such a beautiful blue color. But just as this idea is foolish and contrary to all reason, likewise the outcome of such proposals results in nothing; for it remains the case that without minerals, such a permanent color cannot be produced; as it is said: 

> Seek, and you shall also find something; <note>A variation of the biblical "Seek and ye shall find" (Matthew 7:7).</note>

as I strongly believe that I have perhaps guessed correctly that this blue color has a portion from the mineral kingdom. Should anyone be interested in imitating such a color, I shall herewith give him more instruction for it.

## How to make all kinds of vases and figures from stag horn.

A strong alkaline solution made from wood ashes, used here to break down the collagen in the horn.</note> is poured over it. This is allowed to boil together until it becomes like a porridge or dough. One of the first synthetic pigments, known for its deep intensity.</note>, with which one can make it as blue as one likes; soot, which makes it coal-black. ### 21.
## Regarding a Fixing and Solidifying Oil.

How to make a <term>fixing</term> <note>original: "fixen." In the chemical traditions of the time, to "fix" a substance meant to make a volatile or airy material stable and resistant to the heat of a fire.</note> and <term>solidifying</term> <note>original: "figirenden." Derived from the Latin "figere," meaning to bind or fasten; this refers to the process of turning a liquid or gas into a solid state.</note> <term>oil</term> <note>original: "Oehl."</note>, with which other volatile things can be solidified and bound again, and which also performs its special service for burning.

For this, one takes <term>Venetian soap</term> <note>original: "Venetianische-Seiffe." A high-quality, luxury soap made from olive oil and soda, prized for its purity in chemical experiments.</note>, scrapes it very small, puts it into a tall <term>glass flask</term> <note>original: "Kolben-Glaß." A round-bottomed or long-necked vessel used in laboratories.</note>, and pours strong <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "Spiritus-Vini." High-proof distilled alcohol.</note> over it. Let this stand in gentle warmth, well-secured, for several days; then <term>distill</term> <note>original: "abdistilliren."</note> it over a gentle fire in a <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Capellen." Likely referring here to the heating bath or the vessel within the furnace.</note>. In this way, all the spirit of wine rises over, and it has completely broken down the soap in the flask. During this <term>distillation</term>, however, one must be very cautious that the fire does not become too strong, as otherwise the oil, being a great fattiness, will try to rise up. This is a solidifying oil for <term>volatile ores</term> <note>original: "flüchtige Erze." Ores containing minerals like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that would normally vaporize when heated.</note>, to treat them therein.

<summary>This page concludes a recipe for molding stag horn into polished figures and introduces a method for creating a "fixing oil" using Venetian soap and distilled alcohol. This oil was intended to stabilize volatile minerals and ores during chemical or alchemical processing.</summary>

<keywords>stag horn molding, polishing, fixing oil, Venetian soap, distillation, spirit of wine, volatile ores, alchemy, laboratory equipment</keywords>

### 22.
## A Beautiful Chemical Experiment,
### to dissolve the true Hungarian Vitriol into a blood-red juice, which can also be filtered through paper while remaining blood-red.

One prepares for this work a <term>liquid</term> <note>original: "Liquor." In early chemistry, this refers to a concentrated solution or a fluid substance.</note> made from <term>alkaline salt</term> <note>original: "Sal Alcali."</note> or common <term>potash</term> <note>original: "Potta-aschen." A potassium-rich salt traditionally produced by leaching wood ashes; it was essential for making glass and soap.</note>, specifically so that it is prepared into pieces in the following manner and liquefies on its own when exposed to the air. One pours this alkaline liquid onto crushed <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "Vitriol." A general term for metal sulfates; "Hungarian Vitriol" was often a copper sulfate prized for its purity and supposed gold content.</note> in a glass and lets it stand for one or two days, stirring it constantly; in this way, this salt-liquid will extract a blood-red essence from it, leaving the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "Feces." The solid waste or sediment remaining after a liquid has been extracted or filtered.</note> to settle as a grey mass at the bottom. From this red liquid, a beautiful red <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur." To an alchemist, "sulfur" was not just the yellow element we know today, but a "principle" representing the soul, oiliness, or flammability of a substance.</note> can be <term>precipitated</term> <note>original: "präcipitiret." To cause a solid substance to separate and drop out of a liquid solution.</note>. Specifically, one should dry out such red-colored liquid and dissolve it again in water, into which <term>wine vinegar</term> <note>original: "Wein-Eßig."</note> is poured. From this, the sulfur settles as a beautiful red and, when <term>heated to a glow</term> <note>original: "Ausglüen."</note>, it becomes a beautiful golden yellow.

### 23.
## A Gilder's Base for Gilding.

One grinds yellow <term>ochre</term> <note>original: "Ocher." A natural clay pigment containing iron oxide.</note> and yellow earth

quite finely and clearly on a <term>grinding stone</term> <note>original: "Reib-Stein." A flat stone, often marble or porphyry, used with a muller to grind pigments into a fine paste.</note>, but only with water. Then one grinds such a color with some <term>gum tragacanth</term> <note>original: "Gummi Tragant." A natural gum obtained from the dried sap of legumes; it was a common binder in historical arts and crafts.</note>, and then shaved soap is rubbed into it; in this way, one has a <term>base</term> <note>original: "Grund." The preparatory layer applied to a surface before the gold or silver leaf is added.</note> for gilding with a high luster, which can be polished beautifully and finely. And so that the lower base does not perhaps show through, one must use a yellow color beneath it.

->24.<-

### A Poliment Base for Silvering.

Because one also silvers with a bright finish, a similarly plain, bright, and smooth base must be prepared underneath, just as is done for gold, except that such a base must be white so that no other colors shine through the silver.

In this matter, it is best that the color be ground very small. For the silvering, one takes <term>chalk</term> <note>original: "Kreide." Usually powdered calcium carbonate, used as a filler and white pigment.</note> and <term>lead white</term> <note>original: "Bleyweiß." A heavy, opaque white pigment made from lead; while highly effective for artistic grounds, it is now known to be toxic.</note>; grind these very well and finely with water. When it is nearly dry again, one then grinds it with gum tragacanth and shaved soap so that the color can be polished very smooth; otherwise, neither gold nor silver will lie perfectly flat.

<summary>This page provides recipes for creating smooth preparatory bases (poliment) for gilding and silvering, emphasizing the use of binders like gum tragacanth and soap to achieve a high-polish finish.</summary>
<keywords>gilding, silvering, poliment, gum tragacanth, lead white, chalk, polishing, metal leaf preparation</keywords>

<header>❦ ( 24 ) ❦</header>

### 22.
## A Beautiful Chemical Experiment to dissolve genuine Hungarian Vitriol into a blood-red juice, which can also be filtered blood-red through paper.

For this work, one prepares a <term>liquid</term> <note>original: "Liquor." In early modern chemistry, this refers to a concentrated solution.</note> from <term>alkali salt</term> <note>original: "Sal Alcali." A basic salt, typically potassium carbonate.</note> or common <term>potash</term> <note>original: "Pottaaschen." A substance made from wood ashes used in making glass, soap, and in alchemical processes.</note>, specifically so that it is prepared into pieces in the manner described below and allowed to liquefy on its own in the air. One pours this <term>alkaline liquid</term> onto ground <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "Vitriol." Metal sulfates; "Hungarian Vitriol" usually referred to copper sulfate, which typically yields blue or green colors, making this red reaction particularly striking to the author.</note> in a glass and lets it stand for one or two days, stirring it constantly. In this way, this salt-liquid will extract a blood-red <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Essenz." A concentrated extract or the supposed "pure" part of a substance.</note> from it, leaving the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "Feces." The solid residue or sediment remaining after extraction.</note> to settle at the bottom quite grey. From this red <term>liquid</term>, a beautiful red <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur." In this context, it likely refers to a flammable or colored precipitate rather than elemental sulfur.</note> can be <term>precipitated</term> <note>original: "präcipitiret." To cause a solid substance to separate and drop out of a solution.</note>. Namely, one dries out such red-colored liquid and dissolves it again in water, into which one pours <term>wine vinegar</term>. From this, the <term>sulfur</term> settles into a beautiful red, and upon being calcined, it becomes a beautiful golden-yellow.

### 23.
## A Bolus Base for Gilding.

One grinds yellow <term>ochre</term> <note>original: "Ocher." A natural clay pigment containing iron oxide.</note> and yellow earth

<meta>catchword: thoroughly</meta>

...[grind it] quite finely and clearly on a <term>grinding stone</term> <note>original: "Reib-Stein." A flat stone, typically made of marble or porphyry, used with a muller to grind pigments and binders into a smooth paste.</note>, but only with water: Then one grinds such a color with some <term>gum tragacanth</term> <note>original: "Gummi Tragant." A natural gum obtained from the dried sap of legumes; it acts as a binder that remains flexible when dry.</note>, and then <term>grated soap</term> <note>original: "geschabte Seiffe." Soap was added to the mixture to reduce surface tension and help the gold leaf adhere more smoothly during the burnishing process.</note> is rubbed into it; in this way, one has a base upon which to gild with brilliance, which allows itself to be polished beautifully and finely; and so that the underlying base does not happen to show through, one must use a yellow color beneath it.

### 24.
## A Poliment Base for Silvering.

Since one also silvers to a bright finish with silver, such a plain and bright, smooth base must be prepared underneath, just as with gold, only that such a base must be white, so that no other colors shine through the silver.

In this matter, the best thing is for the color to be ground very small; for the silvering, one takes <term>chalk</term> <note>original: "Kreide." Calcium carbonate, used here as a filler to provide body to the base.</note> and <term>lead white</term> <note>original: "Bleyweiß." A heavy, opaque white pigment made of lead carbonate; while historically essential for its covering power, it is now known to be highly toxic.</note>, grinds them very well and finely with water, and once it is nearly dry again, one then rubs it with gum tragacanth and grated soap, so that the color may be polished very smoothly; otherwise, neither gold nor silver will lie flat and fine.

<note>The vocabulary below highlights the key materials used in 18th-century surface preparation.</note>

<term>Grinding stone</term>, <term>gum tragacanth</term>, <term>soap</term>, <term>poliment base</term> <note>original: "Poliment-Grund." A preparatory layer of clay or pigment that allows metal leaf to be burnished to a high gloss.</note>, <term>silvering</term>, <term>lead white</term>, <term>chalk</term>.

### 25.

## A Good Wood Cement.

When one has large wooden water vats to seal <note>original: "verkütten." To cement, putty, or seal joints to make them watertight.</note>, or other wooden items that must hold water, one must first empty them completely or be able to drain the water entirely. Then, one can melt together a <term>cement</term> <note>original: "Kütte." A putty or sealant made of various binders and fillers.</note> of <term>pitch</term> <note>original: "Pech." A thick, dark resinous substance used for waterproofing.</note>, <term>ox-blood</term> <note>original: "Rinds-Blut." Historically used in mortars and sealants for its protein content, which helped create a durable, waterproof bond.</note>, linseed oil, turpentine, and very finely ground <term>brick dust</term> <note>original: "Ziegel-Mehl." Powdered brick served as a "pozzolanic" filler, strengthening the mixture and helping it withstand moisture.</note> in an iron pan over a fire. The cracks or joints that one wishes to seal should be warmed gently with coals and dried well; then, this cement is poured in, and the joints are stuffed well with <term>tow</term> <note>original: "Werck." Coarse, unspun fibers of flax or hemp used for caulking.</note>; it will be effective. One can also (if one wishes to prepare such a cement properly) take <term>colophony</term> <note>original: "Calphonium." Also known as rosin, a brittle resin obtained from pines, used here to increase the stickiness and hardness of the sealant.</note> and melt it in with the fire.

### 26.

## A Good Stone Cement, to be used hot.

The use of hot stone cements occurs when large stone water vats and <term>pipe-casings</term> <note>original: "Röhr-Kästen." These were stone or wooden boxes used to house pipe joints or serve as small distribution reservoirs in early plumbing systems.</note> that must hold water are to be assembled. Because of the requirement to hold water, and because the stones are naturally cold, this work must be made very warm with fire, so that the stones [take] such cement well

...take hold and remain as firm as iron itself;
for this purpose, the best <term>cement</term> <note>original: "Kütte." A binder or putty used for joining materials.</note> is as follows:

1 <term>hundredweight</term> <note>original: "Centner." A traditional unit of weight, roughly 50 kilograms or 110 pounds depending on the region.</note> <term>pitch</term>, half a hundredweight <term>colophony</term> <note>original: "Calphonium." Rosin or pine resin.</note>,
half a hundredweight <term>minium</term> <note>original: "Minie." Red lead, used as a pigment and a drying agent in cements.</note>, or very fine <term>litharge</term> <note>original: "Silber-Glötte." Lead monoxide, a byproduct of silver refining used to help the mixture harden.</note>, a quarter <term>hundredweight</term>
of <term>white lead</term>, an eighth <term>hundredweight</term> of ground
<term>brick dust</term>, and linseed oil, so that it boils well
into a single mass; then the joints of the stones are
filled by pouring this in, and it holds like iron.

->27.<-
## A Stone Cement, to be used cold.

Using cold <term>stone cement</term> is necessary when
one wants to assemble a column or a <term>statue</term> <note>original: "Bild." Literally "image," but in the context of stonemasonry, it refers to a sculpted figure.</note> from many
pieces of stone, because the stones are often not so large
that one can complete such a work from a single block;
or perhaps even the finest work is broken during
transport and pieces are knocked off; then one
must assist them immediately with a cold cement,
which does its work with great speed.

One takes fresh <term>stone dust</term> <note>original: "Quarck." In this context, it refers to the fine filings or residue created by working the stone.</note> from the
sandstone that one wishes to cement, ground very fine,
and <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: "ungelöſchter Kalck." Calcium oxide, which creates a strong chemical bond when mixed with liquids.</note>; mix these together with
<term>egg white</term> <note>original: "Eyweiß." The proteins in egg white have been used since antiquity as a powerful binding agent in mortars and paints.</note>, and cement the pieces with it;
they will be as strong as iron in just a few minutes.

### 28.

## A Method for Sealing against Water,
## where Cement will not adhere,
## and no other remedy helps.

When one has a cellar or otherwise a floorless <term>vault</term> <note>original: "Gewölbe." Here referring to a subterranean room or cellar structure.</note> that stands upon high water, and the water constantly seeps through, not allowing enough time for a <term>cement</term> <note>original: "Cement." Referring to the binders and putties described on the previous pages.</note> to dry upon it. For no cement can resist water unless it is first completely dry. This problem has already caused many a great headache in similar cases. However, it may be practiced in the following manner:

One has a floor paneled into such cellars using <term>resinous pine</term> <note>original: "Kühnen Holz," likely a variation of *Kienholz* (fatwood). This wood is saturated with natural resin, making it naturally rot-resistant and water-repellent.</note>, and its joints and the entire surface are immediately coated with a brush using a <term>mastic</term> <note>original: "Kitte." A sealant.</note> boiled from <term>pitch</term>, <term>colophony</term> <note>original: "Calphonium." Pine resin or rosin.</note>, tar, and linseed oil, to a thickness of 1 <term>inch</term> <note>original: "Zoll." A traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to 2.5 centimeters.</note>; this holds back the water so that a cement foundation may be laid upon it and allowed to dry out.

❦ ( 29 ) ❦

...boil the <term>turpentine</term> <note>original: "Terpentin." A fluid resin distilled from pine or larch trees, used here as a plasticizer.</note> gently until it begins to smoke thickly; then, add the <term>shellac</term> <note>original: "Gummi-Lack." A resin secreted by the lac bug, which provides the hard, glossy finish of quality sealing wax.</note> into it and stir well with a <term>pestle</term> <note>original: "Reibe-Keule." A grinding tool used to ensure the pigments and resins are perfectly integrated.</note>. Afterward, remove it from the fire and add the <term>cinnabar</term> <note>original: "Zinnober." Mercury sulfide, the traditional pigment used to create the iconic bright red color of official seals.</note>, <term>Balsam of Pegu</term> <note>original: "Balsam de Pegu." A fragrant resin likely sourced from the Pegu region of modern-day Myanmar.</note>, <term>benzoin</term> <note>original: "Benzoe." A balsamic resin from the bark of Styrax trees, added for its pleasant vanilla-like scent when the wax is melted.</note>, and similar ingredients so that it shall smell pleasant, and mix them well together: for all waxes must be made with a gentle heat and be stirred well. However, for the wax sold at 16 <term>Groschen</term> <note>A silver currency unit; this indicates a mid-to-lower grade product.</note> per pound, one must let the turpentine boil strongly so that it does not remain soft. When the turpentine has boiled strongly so that it smokes heavily, then the shellac, or <term>rosin</term> <note>original: "Calphonium." From the Latin *colophonia resina*. A cheaper resin left over from turpentine distillation.</note>, and the chalk are added to it.

## A cold silvering, for metals.

Dissolve 1 Loth of silver in <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Aquafort" or "Scheidewasser." Literally "strong water" or "parting water," this is nitric acid, used by craftsmen to dissolve silver and separate it from gold.</note>; for 1 Loth of silver, [use] 2 Loth

<meta>catchword: parting [Scheide]</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the recipes for colored sealing wax by detailing two versions of green wax using orpiment mixed with blue pigments, and begins a technical instruction for "cold silvering" using nitric acid.</summary>
<keywords>green sealing wax, orpiment, Prussian blue, indigo, cold silvering, aqua fortis, nitric acid, Loth, chemical pigments</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the recipe for "cold silvering" from page 34, completing the list of ingredients and the application method before beginning a new recipe for "fire silvering" as practiced by professional smiths.</meta>

parting water <note>original: "Scheidewaſſer." Nitric acid, used to separate silver from gold.</note>, and when everything has dissolved, place thin little copper sheets into it, and thus let the silver precipitate and settle into a fine lime <note>original: "subtilen Kalck." In this period, "lime" (calx) referred to any metal reduced to a powder through chemical action.</note>. For it is useful to know that copper displaces silver from <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Aquafort." Also nitric acid.</note>. Iron [displaces] copper; iron precipitates laminated copper. Mercury or quicksilver [is precipitated by] <term>spirit of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Vitrioli." Sulfuric acid.</note> and <term>oil of tartar through deliquescence</term> <note>original: "Oleum Tartari per deliquium." A concentrated solution of potassium carbonate made by allowing the salt to absorb moisture from the air.</note>. Tin is precipitated by oil of tartar (it makes a great noise <note>likely referring to a vigorous effervescent reaction</note>). Lead is precipitated by oil of tartar and creates a white powder. Take 1 <term>Loth</term> <note>A traditional German unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams.</note> of this precipitated silver lime, 2 Loth of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>Ammonium chloride.</note>, 2 Loth of <term>rock salt</term> <note>original: "Sal gemmæ." Pure salt crystals.</note>, [and] 1/2 Loth of "living" quicksilver <note>Liquid mercury.</note>. Rub these well together and thus make them into a powder. If you wish to silver something with this, whether it be copper or brass, moisten the powder slightly and rub it onto the metal. When it is dry, rub it clean with a woolen cloth; then it will look beautiful and white, like the finest silver.

### 33.

## The best fire-gilding for goldsmiths, belt-makers, and sword-furbishers.

Take thinly beaten <term>ducats</term> <note>Gold coins prized for their high purity.</note>, cut them together to the weight of 1 <term>Loth</term> <note>A traditional unit of weight, roughly 14.6 grams.</note>, and place them in a small melting crucible in the fire until the crucible glows only a brownish-red. Then, lift it out and place it separately on a stone, and then add 5, 6, 8, or more <term>Loth</term> of mercury to it—depending on whether the gilding should be thick or thin—and likewise stir it together with a small oaken stick (just as was taught for the silvering) until you feel that the gold has united with the mercury. Then pour it from the crucible into water, so that an <term>amalgam</term> is likewise formed from it. This must

Now, when one has silver vessels or similar items to be gilded, one must proceed with them as was taught regarding silvering: namely, that after the <term>gilding</term> <note>The process of applying a thin layer of gold.</note>, all the <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius"</note> must be smoked off <note>evaporated using heat</note> again, and only then is it first polished. The superior <note>meaning thicker or higher quality</note> the gilding on such silverwork is intended to be, the more gold one must add, and the less mercury one must use in the <term>amalgamation</term> <note>The process of mixing mercury with another metal to create a paste.</note>. For 1 <term>Loth</term> <note>A traditional unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams.</note> of gold, 4 <term>Loth</term> of mercury provides nearly the highest quality gilding; for the mercury requires this amount for its dissolution and becomes a thick paste with it. But 1 <term>Loth</term> of gold and 10 to 12 <term>Loth</term> of mercury is the lightest and poorest gilding; it does not cost as much, but must be treated and worked in the same manner.

## Note.

Regarding the "smoking off" of the mercury or quicksilver, I wish here to inform the careless or the ignorant that this must be done very carefully and cautiously, as there is danger to life and limb during such work because of the mercurial fumes. A few years ago in <term>Hirschberg</term> <note>Modern-day Jelenia Góra, Poland</note> in <term>Silesia</term> <note>A historical region in Central Europe, now mostly in Poland.</note>, two goldsmiths' apprentices lost their lives in a careless manner because of it; therefore, such work should be done in the open air,

[should be done], or under such a hearth-chimney that has a very good upward draft, and one must always stand behind the smoke so that one does not inhale any of it, for the fumes of <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurii"</note> are deadly.

### 34.
## To make the gold for this work—namely for gilding and for whatever else one desires it—super-fine.

One takes 1 part <term>gold</term> and 4 parts <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonium" or "Spieß-Glas". This refers to stibnite, the naturally occurring sulfide of antimony used here to purify gold.</note>, melts these together in a <term>crucible</term> <note>original: "Schmelz-Tiegel"</note>, and adds some iron to it, so that during the melting the gold may <term>precipitate</term> <note>original: "präcipitire"</note>; and as the <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> attacks the iron, it allows the gold to fall to the bottom in a <term>king</term> <note>original: "König"; a metallurgical term for the pure metal button found at the bottom of a crucible after smelting.</note>. As soon as the gold and the antimony are well melted together, one pours this out into an iron or brass <term>casting mold</term> <note>original: "Gieß-Puckel"; a conical ingot mold used to separate the metal from the slag.</note>, which must have been previously well-greased with fat or tallow and pre-heated. When such a casting mold has stood for a little while, one taps it with tongs or a hammer so that the <term>regulus</term> <note>original: "Regulus"; the metallic mass that collects at the bottom of the mold.</note> with the gold detaches nicely and falls to the bottom. Afterwards, when everything has cooled, one turns the casting mold over and knocks off the <term>regulus</term> with the gold, and sets the knocked-off <term>slag</term> <note>original: "Schlacken"</note>

<summary>This page concludes safety warnings about toxic mercury vapors and begins a technical instruction on refining gold using antimony and iron in a crucible to produce a high-purity metal button.</summary>
<keywords>mercury fumes, safety, gold refining, antimony, crucible, precipitation, regulus, casting mold, metallurgy</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for purifying gold using antimony. The previous page described the initial melting process where gold is separated from impurities to form a "regulus" or metallic button at the bottom of a casting mold.</meta>

over again, adds 3 times as much fresh **antimony** <note>original: "Antimonium"</note> by weight, and lets the same melt together once more, as it often happens that in the first melting not all the gold **precipitates** <note>original: "präcipitiret"</note> at once into the **regulus** <note>original: "Regulum"; the metallic mass that collects at the bottom of a mold</note>. Therefore, it must be melted perhaps 2 more times with some fresh antimony, poured again into the **casting mold** <note>original: "Gieß-Puckel"; a conical mold used to separate metal from slag</note>, and the regulus struck off, until no more falls to the bottom. One then collects these **kings** <note>original: "Könige"; a traditional metallurgical term for the pure metal buttons found at the bottom of a crucible</note> together and, on a **refining dish** <note>original: "Trieb-Scherben"; a shallow, heat-resistant ceramic dish used in cupellation</note>, uses a small **hand bellows** <note>original: "Hand-Balgen"</note> to blow off or smoke away the antimony, so that the gold remains on the dish; this same gold is then fully melted together with a little **borax** <note>A flux used to prevent oxidation and aid melting</note> until it can withstand the blowing. This gold is now as super-fine as any in the world can be, and is used with great benefit especially in **medicine**, and for other things as well.

One takes 1 **loth** <note>A historical unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams or half an ounce</note> of **ducat gold** <note>High-purity gold from ducat coins</note>, 4 loth of copper, and melts these together

in a good and pure **crucible** <term>Schmelz-Tiegel; a heat-resistant ceramic container</term> in a strong **wind-furnace** <term>Wind-Ofen; a furnace designed with a strong draft to reach the high temperatures required for melting metals</term> that draws well; (for copper and gold both require a strong fire for melting); then poured into a well-greased **casting mold** <term>Einguß; a mold used to shape the molten metal as it cools</term>, and allowed to cool. refurbished and made fine again, whether they are sewn on or detached:

Take 3 **Loth** <term>Loth; a historical Germanic unit of weight, approximately 14–15 grams or half an ounce</term> of **shellac** <term>Gummi-Lack; a resin secreted by the lac insect, used here as a varnish</term>, half a **quintlein** <term>Quintlein; a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 to 4 grams</term> of **dragon's blood** <term>Drachen-Blut; a bright red resin obtained from certain plants, used for coloring</term>, and half a **quintlein** of **turmeric root** <term>Curcume</term>. The same can be done with **embroidered work** <term>aufgestickte Arbeit</term>. If, however, you have **gold buttons** <term>goldene Knöpffe</term> that have been cut off, put them on a fork, brush them with this extract, and afterwards hold them over a **charcoal fire** <term>Kohlfeuer</term>, though at a slight distance until they are dry; thus they will become beautiful again.

### 38.
## To prepare a precious stain soap yourself, with which to remove all stains from clothes.

Take a quarter-pound of **Venetian soap** <note>A high-quality olive oil soap originally from Venice</note>, a half-pound of **Venetian turpentine** <note>A resin collected from the larch tree</note>, 4 **Loth** of **white vitriol** <term>Galitzen-Stein; zinc sulfate, used historically in cleaning and as a mordant</term>, 4 **Loth** of **salt of tartar** <term>Sal-Tartari; potassium carbonate, a strong alkaline cleaning agent</term>, and a quarter of an **ox gall** <note>The bile of an ox, prized for its ability to dissolve fats and stains</note>. Shave the Venetian soap finely, crush the salt of tartar and white vitriol, and mix it all together until everything is absorbed. Then form **soap balls** <term>Seiffen-Kugeln</term> from it. Wet the stain

### 39.
### A quick method for making a water to remove all stains.

One takes **potash** <term>Pottaschen; potassium carbonate</term>, dissolves it in cold water, and filters it through **blotting paper** <term>Lösch-Pappier</term>. To this, pour **liquor of salt of tartar** <term>Liqvor von Weinstein-Salz</term>—specifically the liquid that forms when the salt is left to dissolve in the moisture of the air—then squeeze the juice of several **rotten lemons** <note>The citric acid in lemons, even those beginning to decay, acted as a powerful natural bleaching agent and solvent</note> into it, and shake it all together well. Smear any stain on a garment with this water (even if it is made of **silk fabric**) and finally wash it out again with fresh water; in this way, it will become quite clean.

### 40.
### To make the finest English Tombac, equal to gold in color, which does not tarnish and is also supple for chasing.

To make the true **English Tombac** <note>Tombac is a brass alloy with a high copper content, often used to imitate gold in jewelry and clockmaking</note> is still a secret art, and has always been **kept very secret** <note>original: "geheim gehal-"; the word is cut off by the page break</note> by those who are able to manufacture it—

—been kept, because it far exceeds the common **tombac** <note>Tombac is a high-copper brass alloy used to imitate gold</note> made nowadays in its lasting color and virtue, and except for the weight, it is indistinguishable from gold. It also exceeds other tombac in weight, and a pound is still paid for at 10 **Imperial Thalers** <term>Reichs-Thaler; the standard large silver coin of the Holy Roman Empire</term> at any time, because it is not manufactured from coarse metals like the other. Since this treatise <note>original: "Tractat"</note> is intended to contain only genuine pieces, I shall quite sincerely disclose it here, along with all pieces that are particularly highly valued and can be immediately imitated by a skilled hand:

One makes an **extraction** <term>Extraction; the process of drawing out specific substances using a solvent</term> from 1 pound of **French verdigris** <term>Grünspan; a green pigment or crust formed on copper, often used in alchemy</term> and several measures of good **wine vinegar**. Add to this 1 pound of common salt, 1 pound of **Hungarian vitriol** <term>Ungarischen Vitriol; a sulfate mineral, likely copper or iron sulfate</term>, and 1 pound of **quicksilver** <note>original: "Mercurium vivum"</note>, and boil these substances together in an iron crucible. Stir with a wooden spatula until the mercury has "hidden itself" <note>This refers to the mercury reacting or being absorbed into the mixture until it is no longer visible as silver liquid beads</note> and has become a dry powder. 

Then one adds to it a half pound of **turmeric** <term>Corcome; a yellow root used here for its coloring properties</term> and 1 pound of **Alexandrian tutty** <term>Tutia Alexandrina; a crude form of zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</term> (ensuring everything is very well pulverized). Afterward, pour this material together into a stone **retort** <term>Retorte; a vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distillation</term> and place a glass receiver in front with some water added, so that the remaining mercury can rise and collect in it. Then, heat it gradually <note>original: "gradatim"</note> and finally with strong

...fire, the **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium"</note> has been separated from it without harm to health, which is otherwise dangerous if this work were to be handled in a **luted** <term>verlutirten; sealed with a clay or paste called "lute" to make it airtight</term> melting crucible. As soon as the distillation is over, one takes out the **retort** <term>Retorte; a glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term>, breaks it apart, and melts the **tombac** <term>Tombac; a high-copper brass alloy used to imitate gold</term> completely together in a melting crucible. It is then as beautiful as gold in color and so **malleable** <note>original: "geschmeidig"</note> besides that it may be hammered and drawn into wire. The mercury that has passed over <note>This refers to the mercury that evaporated and re-condensed in the receiver</note> can be used again for other work.

->41.<-

### To manufacture white tombac, 
### which can be hammered as 
### finely as silver and used 
### for beautiful work.

For the white tombac, one only needs to leave out the **turmeric** <term>Corcomeé; a yellow plant root used as a dye</term> and the **tutty** <term>Tutia; a crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term>; in this way, one obtains a completely white metal. For the tutty, by its nature, tints the copper-like quality of the **verdigris** <term>Grünspan; the green crust that forms on copper</term> and **Hungarian vitriol** <term>Ungarischen Vitriols; likely copper(II) sulfate in this context</term> into yellow. Although this tombac is already costly to make because of its preparation, it nevertheless finds its admirers, as it does not tarnish nor turn green, and is moreover quite malleable. I shall not list the inferior tombacs made from copper and brass here—

<summary>This page concludes the process for creating a gold-colored tombac through distillation and introduces a variation for "white tombac" by omitting coloring agents. The author emphasizes the metal's durability, malleability, and resistance to tarnishing compared to cheaper imitations.</summary>

<keywords>mercury, distillation, retort, tombac, white tombac, turmeric, tutty, verdigris, vitriol, metallurgy, alchemy</keywords>

### 42.

## To melt tin and brass together very smoothly and to cast all sorts of white metal objects from them.

This is a truly marvelous work, and many to whom I have spoken of it have not believed it; yet it is entirely certain and sure, as a trial <note>The author uses "Probe" (trial/test) to emphasize empirical proof, a common trope in early modern technical manuals.</note> will demonstrate to anyone.

One takes a tin-plated casting ladle <note>original: "blechernen Gieß-Löffel"</note>, allows half a pound of tin to melt in it over a charcoal fire, then removes it from the fire and places it in another cool, convenient spot (the brass melts within it while away from the fire). Then one should buy six pennies' worth of olive oil <term>Baumöhl; literally "tree oil," the common historical term for vegetable oil, usually olive oil</term> and six pennies' worth of spirit of wine <term>Spiritus Vini; a high-proof distilled alcohol, similar to modern brandy or ethanol</term>, pour them together, and swirl them well (even though the olive oil otherwise does not mix with the spirit of wine, but constantly floats on top). With this swirled mixture, one brushes brass buttons, picks them up with a fork, and dips them into the hot, liquid tin; thus the brass button will, away from the fire and to great astonishment, instantly melt and flow away—

<summary>This page describes a "marvelous" metallurgical technique for alloying brass into molten tin outside of a furnace using a mixture of oil and alcohol as a flux. The author notes that this process is often met with skepticism but insists on its efficacy for casting white metal objects.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, brass, tin, alloy, white metal, spirit of wine, olive oil, casting, flux</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions from page 47 regarding the surprising chemical reaction that allows brass to melt into tin at low temperatures using a specific flux.</meta>

...so that nothing but the iron handle will remain on the fork. This occurs despite the fact that it is well known that brass requires a very intense fire to melt, and that tin and brass do not normally combine during melting to form a pliable mass. Should the tin begin to solidify <note>The author uses the phrase "in seinem Fluß stehen wollen," referring to the metal losing its fluidity or "standing" still.</note>, you can always place the casting ladle back over the fire and, while it is liquid, pour some of the olive oil <term>Baumöhl; historically "tree oil," usually referring to olive oil</term> and spirit of wine <term>Spiritus-Vini; a high-proof distilled alcohol</term> into it. Although it will immediately burn away with a large flame, it creates a supple and soft mass. In this manner, you can add as many old brass buttons and other items as you desire.

### 43.

## To remove tin from tin-plated iron quickly, without spending a penny, without melting the iron, and without damaging the workmanship.

It often happens that something breaks on tin-plated ironware—such as horse bridles and various other items—which cannot be welded <note>original: "geschweisset"</note> back together because the presence of tin prevents the bond. Few locksmiths <note>original: "Schlosser"</note> or blacksmiths <note>original: "Schmiede"</note> will be found who know how to remedy this situation. Indeed, entire chests full of old tin-plated iron are often sorted out <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with instructions on how to strip the tin to make the iron workable again.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a recipe for alloying brass and tin using a flammable flux and begins a new instruction on how to strip tin from iron salvage so it can be repaired or welded.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, brass, tin, flux, olive oil, spirit of wine, blacksmithing, welding, tin-plating, recycling iron</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions from page 48 regarding the salvaging of tin-plated iron that has been discarded because it could not be easily repaired or welded.</meta>

and set aside, since it is no longer fit for remelting or any other use. This problem can be remedied quickly:

Namely, heat the tin-plated iron until it glows red, then rub it with a piece of horn or a cow’s hoof <note>The organic material of the horn or hoof likely acts as a flux, helping the tin to release from the iron surface.</note>. The tin will immediately run off, leaving the iron clean so that it can then be welded <note>original: "geschweisset"</note> and forged as one wishes—something that was impossible before this simple trick.

### 44.
### A boiling-white bath for whitening needles.
<note>In the early modern period, an "Ansud" was a chemical bath used to give metal objects like needles or pins a bright, silver-like coating of tin.</note>

Take English tin <note>original: "Englisch Zinn"; English tin was considered the highest quality and most pure during this era.</note>, and let it melt in a crucible over the fire. To one pound of tin, add 4 Loth <term>Loth; a historical German weight, roughly equivalent to 14–17 grams or half an ounce</term> of sal ammoniac <term>Salmiac; ammonium chloride, used as a flux to clean the metal</term> little by little. Burn it off well with grease <term>Schmeer; rendered animal fat or lard</term>, and then grain it in a trough that has first been well-coated with chalk. Melt it again in a crucible and burn it off with grease once more. Finally, when it has been tossed into fine granules, the tin is properly prepared for the boiling and plating of brass and copper.

<summary>This page provides a clever workshop trick for removing tin from iron using a cow's hoof and details the preparation of a granulated tin mixture for whitening brass and copper needles.</summary>
<keywords>tin removal, iron welding, salvage, English tin, sal ammoniac, graining metal, needle making, tinning bath</keywords>

### 45.
## To examine a true and genuine gold streak on the touchstone.

Since today not all that glitters is gold, and frequently a finely crafted <term>tombac</term> <note>a brass alloy with high copper content, often used as "simulated gold" in jewelry and horology</note> matches the color of the most beautiful gold and cannot be rightly recognized by everyone, I will teach how to examine it here, so that no one is deceived by it:

One should take <term>verdigris</term> <note>green pigment or crust formed on copper or brass; original: "Grünspan"</note> and <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride, a common flux and cleaning agent; original: "Salmiac"</note>, 4 <term>Loth</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce</note> of each; and <term>burnt copper</term> <note>original: "Aes uftum"; copper oxidized by heating to a high temperature</note> and <term>saltpeter</term>, 1 <term>Quintslein</term> <note>a small weight, approximately 3.75 to 4 grams</note> of each. These pieces should be ground very fine and mixed together, then rubbed well with wine vinegar. With this mixture, one smears the gold streak <note>The "streak" refers to the mark left by rubbing the metal against a dark, slightly abrasive "touchstone" to test its quality.</note>; if it is silver or <term>tombac</term>, it will vanish and nothing will remain except the genuine gold streak.

### 46.
## To examine the fine silver streak on the touchstone.

Fine silver, as well as scrap silver, can no longer be identified today simply by the streak on the stone, because white copper <note>likely "paktong" or a similar copper alloy containing arsenic or nickel, which mimics the appearance of silver</note> is often mixed in, which then frequently [makes up] the 10th or 12th [part]...

also produces a 16-<term>Loth</term> streak <note>A "16-Loth" streak refers to a mark indicating pure silver; in the historical German system, 16 Loth represented the highest purity of silver.</note> when tested against silver, and yet is nothing but a white metal that does not withstand the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Capelle"; a small, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals through high heat</note>, but instead flies away. If one coats a silver streak with this water, the silver must remain if it is genuine, but gold and copper will go away and disappear.

->47.<-

## How to remove gold from gilded copper and brass dishes and vessels without causing them harm or damage.

One should make a thick paste from <term>borax</term> and water (specifically, after the borax has been boiled beforehand and has lost its effervescence and rising). Apply this borax to the gilded areas with a brush, but no further than where the gold lies. Sprinkle these coated areas with powdered <term>sulfur</term>, and hold the vessels over a

...glow of the fire until they are red-hot, then placed into a vessel with warm water. This gold is then fully <term>reduced</term> <note>A metallurgical process where a metal is restored to its pure, metallic state from a compound or oxide.</note> with a little <term>borax</term> and melted into fine gold in a <term>crucible</term>.

### 48.
## How to remove gold from silver vessels without damaging them.

That this is also a very useful technique, well-suited for the use of goldsmiths and <term>refiners</term> <note>original: "Scheider"; literally "separators," these were specialists who separated precious metals from base metals or from each other.</note>, is self-evident:

One takes very finely ground <term>tartar</term> <note>original: "Weinstein"; a sediment from wine production (potassium bitartrate) used frequently in early chemistry and metal cleaning.</note> and boils it in strong wine vinegar. Place the gilded vessels into this solution; then, after they are removed, coat them with <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Mercurium-Vivum"; liquid mercury.</note> and rub them with powdered <term>vitriol</term> <note>a historical term for various metal sulfates.</note>. By doing this, the gold will fall away entirely along with the mercury. The mercury is then pressed through a piece of leather <note>Squeezing through chamois or fine leather was a common way to filter liquid mercury, leaving the solid gold-mercury amalgam behind.</note> to separate it from the gold. The remaining gold is melted with borax and thus reduced.

### To distill the true sweet <term>spirit of salt</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Salis"; the historical name for hydrochloric acid.</note>, to unlock gold with it, and to use it as medicine.

For a long time, many have endeavored to dissolve gold using spirit of salt and to use it for medicine, as mentions of this appear now and again in the writings of the <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymicis"; referring to alchemists or early chemists.</note>; but this could not be accomplished using the well-known distilled spirit of salt made by <term>lab workers</term> <note>original: "Laboranten"; individuals who prepared chemical products for commercial sale.</note> and brought to market.
