The salt crystals are then driven again in an open fire from a stone <term>retort</term> <note>A glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances.</note>; thus one obtains the true and sweet <term>Spirit of Salt</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Salis"; historically refers to hydrochloric acid, though in this alchemical context, it is described as "sweet" and medicinal.</note>, which can dissolve a prepared <term>calx of gold</term> <note>original: "Gold-Kalck"; gold that has been chemically reduced to a fine powder.</note> and produce a <term>tincture</term> or <term>essence</term> from it for human health.

## 51.

### A special art for gilding silver
### vessels with 1 gold ducat better
### and more richly than
### another might with 2 ducats.

One should prepare a <term>Crocus of Venus</term> <note>original: "Crocum Veneris"; a reddish oxide of copper used as a pigment or alchemical reagent.</note> and <term>reverberate</term> <note>To heat a substance in a furnace so that the flames are deflected onto it, often to oxidize it.</note> it in an open flame until it has become blood-red and proper. This <term>Crocus</term> is then <term>amalgamated</term> <note>Mixed into a paste with mercury.</note> with <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Mercurium Vivum"; liquid mercury.</note>. With this <term>amalgam</term>, one carries out the gilding; in this way, it will become red and golden-shimmering throughout, which otherwise does not happen with mercury alone, for mercury is white. In this manner, one can gild with beaten leaf-gold, and it does not necessarily have to be powdered gold.

...has been cast three times through **Antimony** <note>original: "Antimonium"; a semi-metal used in alchemy to refine gold, as it draws away impurities like a "metallurgical soap."</note>; for it not only purifies itself from all other volatile metallic natures but also raises itself in its own degree <note>The "degree" refers to the purity or "fineness" of the gold.</note>, which one clearly notices and can utilize in such work. Therefore, gold is mostly taken as it has been minted into ducats <note>Standard gold coins of the period, known for their high purity.</note>, and placed immediately into **Aqua Regia** <note>original: "Aqua-Regis," Latin for "Royal Water"; a powerful mixture of acids capable of dissolving gold.</note>. If one wishes to gild silverwork quickly and while cold, rub the polished silver piece [with this powder] in fresh water, and a beautiful gilding will appear. The **Aqua Regia** for this work is also easy to prepare: take several <term>Loth</term> <note>A historical unit of weight, approximately 15-16 grams or half an ounce.</note> of <term>parting-water</term> <note>original: "Scheide-Waſſer" or "Aquafort" (Aqua Fortis); nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver.</note>, add half a Loth of powdered **Salmiac** <note>Ammonium chloride; adding this to nitric acid creates Aqua Regia.</note> into it, and let it dissolve; then it is prepared.

Because the glowing <note>This refers to the process of annealing: heating the metal to a specific temperature to relieve internal stresses and restore its flexibility (suppleness) after it has become brittle from being pulled through the drawing-dies.</note> of the gold during **gold wire-drawing** consists of a specific technique that not every **gold and silver wire-drawer** knows; however, this glowing not only enhances the gold but also, if not carefully observed, spoils the entire work and makes it spotted, because such glowing work occurs when the wire is already in the gauges <note>original: "Nommern" (numbers); referring to the specific drawing stages or thickness levels of the wire.</note>—that is, 6 to 7—and is thus already delicate, which is why few wire-drawers attempt it.

# On Composition-Work.

**Composition** <term>composition</term> or **stone-work** <term>stone-work</term> <note>original: "Composition oder Stein-Arbeit"; the craft of making high-quality glass imitations of gems, also known as "paste."</note> is

**precious stones** <term>precious stones</term> <note>original: "Edel-Gesteine"</note>, in their color=

Bohemia carries on a massive trade from this work, and such colored **composition stones** <term>composition stones</term> <note>Artificial gemstones made from "paste" or specialized lead glass to mimic rubies, emeralds, and other jewels.</note> are sent not only to all places in Germany, but also to France, Spain, and other kingdoms; in the small town of **Turnov** <note>original: "Tornau"; a town in Northern Bohemia (modern-day Czech Republic) that remains famous for its gemstone cutting and jewelry heritage.</note> alone, there are about 300 stone-cutters who make, grind, and cut these imitated and smelted stones.

But this composition is melted in great quantities by such people, and indeed in this way: They place these compositions in large **Hessian melting crucibles** <term>Hessian crucibles</term> <note>Heat-resistant ceramic vessels made from Almerode clay in Hesse, prized by alchemists and glassmakers throughout Europe.</note>—which cost up to 34 **Böhm** <note>A Bohemian currency unit.</note> each there—inside larger crucibles, 15 to 18 of which [are placed] into a **lime kiln** <term>lime kiln</term> <note>original: "Kalck-Ofen"; the heat from the industrial lime-burning process was used simultaneously to melt the glass composition, a clever use of fuel.</note> along with the lime when it is to be burned. ## Concerning the Composition of these Imitation Gemstones in Particular

The nature and properties of these imitation gemstones are as varied as the authentic types of gemstones; however, they all have as their **body** <term>body</term> <note>original: "Corpus"; refers to the base material or matrix of the gemstone composition.</note> the common **pebble** <term>pebble</term> <note>original: "Kieselstein"; likely flint or quartz pebbles used as the source of silica for glassmaking.</note>, which, through such melting, acquires the clarity of the most beautiful and transparent **crystal** <term>crystal</term> <note>original: "Crystall"; here referring to "lead crystal" or high-quality glass rather than natural rock crystal.</note>. Whatever colors are introduced into such crystal, it adopts them and attains that color. Therefore, it is entirely possible to imitate all colored gemstones in this manner, as will be clearly understood in what follows; however, for the sake of clarity, I wish to mention the preparation of the pebble for such work.

The pebble, as it is found in the **mountains** <note>original: "Gebürgen"</note> and in the fields or forests, is of a very firm and rigid body, which refuses to yield to the hammer or to pounding in a **mortar** <term>mortar</term> <note>original: "Mörsel"</note>, nor will it allow itself to be made into a fine powder—even though such a state is absolutely required for this work. Therefore, one must take recourse in fire, and

<summary>This page explains that all imitation gemstones share a common base material—silica from pebbles—which can be colored to mimic any natural stone. It notes that raw pebbles are too hard to grind and require heat treatment to be processed into powder.</summary>
<keywords>imitation gemstones, silica, flint pebbles, material preparation, crystal glass, gemstone composition</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the preparation of pebbles (silica) for glassmaking. It follows the description of why fire is necessary to break down the hard structure of the stone so it can be ground into a fine powder for imitation gemstones.</meta>

( 67 )
E 2

...seek to conquer such firm stones with it.
One should therefore set up a furnace, just like a lime kiln, filled with such pebbles, to **calcine** <term>calcine</term> <note>original: "calciniren"; heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile substances or to make it brittle.</note> them and fire them properly, particularly if one intends to make large **batches** <term>batches</term> <note>original: "Compositiones"</note> and entire firings of such stones. Otherwise, the calcination can also take place in smaller **draft furnaces** <term>draft furnaces</term> <note>original: "Wind-Oefen"</note>, and thus be quenched in cold water until they are quite soft and easy to conquer with the mortar; however, one must take great care when selecting such pebbles that they are all beautiful, white, and pure, and not mixed with any metallic or mineral stains, for otherwise they would ruin whatever color was applied to them and take on a muddied *color* <note>original: "Couleur"</note>, especially if they were intended to represent white or the color of a **topaz** <note>original: "Topasis"</note>; for copper-like and iron-bearing qualities and properties are very easily found in such pebbles.

## Remark.

All these gemstones receive their colors from metals, and thus within the earth, through the influence of their **celestial influences** <term>celestial influences</term> <note>original: "Astrorum"; in alchemy, this refers to the astrological or spiritual forces believed to govern the formation of minerals.</note>; therefore, one can restore these colors to the imitation stones, or the so-called **composition** <term>composition</term> <note>original: "Composition"</note>, using metals matured by nature, namely when they have been dissolved and their coloring and tinting **sulfur** <note>The text ends here with the catchword "Sulphur", referring to the alchemical principle of Sulfur, often associated with color and combustibility.</note>

<summary>This page describes the process of calcining and quenching pebbles to make them brittle enough for grinding, while warning that impurities like iron or copper can ruin the clarity of the resulting glass. It also introduces the alchemical theory that gemstones derive their colors from metallic "influences" within the earth.</summary>

...**sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur"; in alchemical theory, sulfur was the principle of "combustibility" and "color" within a substance.</note> is discovered in them; as indeed natural experience demonstrates in such stones. **Garnets**, **rubies**, and **carbuncles** <note>A "carbuncle" historically referred to any deep red gemstone, usually a garnet cut into a rounded cabochon shape.</note> derive their colors from the **Star of the Sun** <term>Star of the Sun</term> <note>original: "Astro-Solis"; in alchemy, the sun is the celestial counterpart to the metal gold.</note>, and are therefore subject to **gold**; thus, to imitate them, its sulfur is required—whether that same sulfur might be found and prepared in other things more readily than in gold itself.

**Sapphire-blue** comes from the sky-blue **Moon** <term>Moon</term> <note>original: "Luna"; the alchemical name for silver.</note>; therefore, **cobalt** is subject to its birth-stars, and from it this color can be given to the **batch** <term>batch</term> <note>original: "Composition"; the mixture of ingredients used to create artificial gemstones or glass.</note>.

**Emeralds** and **chrysolites** <note>In historical texts, "chrysolite" often referred to peridot or other yellowish-green gems.</note> come from the green **Venus** <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "Venus"; the alchemical name for copper, which produces green pigments.</note>; it is thus shown that such a color can be returned to the stones through it. Because the chrysolite has a yellowish shimmer, one recognizes how **Mars** <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Mars"; the alchemical name for iron.</note> and Venus are mixed therein, and thus its color can be incorporated into such a stone through them.

**Topaz** and **hyacinth** <note>A "hyacinth" or jacinth is a yellow, orange, or reddish-brown variety of zircon.</note> take their color from Mars and its **stars** <term>stars</term> <note>original: "Astris"; the celestial influences or astrological forces believed to govern the formation and properties of minerals.</note>; through its **calcined oxide** <term>calcined oxide</term> <note>original: "Crocum"; specifically *Crocus Martis*, a red or yellow powder made from iron used as a pigment.</note> such stones can also be imitated, all of which shall be taught step-by-step in the following processes.

Therefore, a white, transparent stone is quite easily made from the minium and the silica.

## 58.
## To make lead salt or lead crystals for this purpose.

One beats lead thin into small **lamellae** <note>original: "Lamellen"; thin, foil-like plates of metal to increase surface area for chemical reactions.</note>, then cuts these into very small pieces, puts them into a **dissolving glass** <term>dissolving glass</term> <note>original: "Solvir-Glas"; a glass vessel, such as a flask, used for dissolving substances in acid.</note> and pours good **aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Scheide-Wasser"; literally "parting water," the historical name for nitric acid, used to separate or "part" metals.</note> over it, so that the lead dissolves therein. Then, one draws the water off or lets it evaporate over the fire until the **crystallization point** <note>original: "Anschuß"; the stage of evaporation where a concentrated solution begins to form solid crystals.</note>; then lead crystals will form, which can now be made in quantity. The **compositions** <term>compositions</term> <note>original: "Compositions"; the specific chemical recipes for glass pastes used to imitate gems.</note> made with such lead salt or lead crystals remain very pure and beautiful, and they also become clear during melting.

A small barrel of water is placed underneath, and the melted steel is allowed to run into it, which becomes nothing but hollow little spheres; these can easily be crushed into a powder in a **mortar** <term>mortar</term> <note>original: "Mörsel".</note> and look like gold. This glowing process is then repeated until the steel is all gone. ## 61.
# How a ruby is further prepared.

Take six parts **antimony** <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonium"; here likely the mineral stibnite.</note>, four parts **orpiment** <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "Auri pigmentum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment.</note>, three parts **arsenic** <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arsenicum".</note>, three parts sulfur, and two parts **Alexandrian tutty** <term>Alexandrian tutty</term> <note>original: "Tutia Alexandrina"; an impure form of zinc oxide collected from the chimneys of smelting furnaces, used in metallurgy and alchemy.</note>. All of this should be pounded very clearly and finely, and passed through a sieve. Now, take beautiful pieces of crystal, or work that is already finished, and place them into a melting crucible. **Stratify** <term>stratify</term> <note>original: "stratificiret"; a process of layering the objects with the chemical powder, also known as cementation.</note> them with this powder, place it in the fire, and let it glow thoroughly for 5 to 6 hours—but only in a charcoal fire and without using bellows, otherwise they will burst. (For this work requires a steady and quiet fire.) Then, one must also let the stones together with the crucible cool down completely; otherwise, they will crack again and be damaged. After this, one can see if the pieces have turned out well; if not, set them in again and let them glow for a while longer, until you believe it to be right.

<summary>This page describes two methods for producing artificial rubies: first by "striking" a gold-yellow glass melt into a red color, and second by using a "cementation" process where crystal pieces are layered with a reactive mineral powder and heated.</summary>
<keywords>ruby glass, glassmaking, antimony, orpiment, tutty, stratification, cementation, salt of tartar, striking glass</keywords>

### 62.
## To make crystal as red as a ruby.

One takes well-rotted **urine** <note>Urine was a vital source of ammonia and phosphates in early modern chemistry and was frequently used as a leaching agent or solvent.</note> from a twelve-year-old boy and lets it be cleanly filtered. Then, the **crystal** <term>crystal</term> <note>original: "Crystall"; likely referring here to clear quartz or high-quality flint glass.</note> is placed into a well-sealed flask or a cucurbit, and a **blind head** <term>blind head</term> <note>original: "blinden Helm"; a still-head without a spout or "beak," used to keep vapors within the vessel so they condense and fall back down—a process known as refluxing.</note> is set upon it. Let it stand thus for a quarter of a year, and afterwards boil it all together; in this way, it acquires a blood-red color and allows itself to be cut like any other stone.

### 63.
## To imitate the garnet.

If one wishes to have the ruby-flux darker and in the manner of a **garnet** <term>garnet</term> <note>original: "Granat"; a deep red gemstone. In this context, the author is describing a "flux" or lead-glass paste used to create costume jewelry.</note>, one must proceed as follows:

Take one part of very clearly ground **flint** <term>flint</term> <note>original: "Kiesel"; used as the source of silica for glassmaking.</note> and three parts of **red lead** <term>red lead</term> <note>original: "rothe Mennige"; also known as minium, this lead oxide acts as a flux to lower the melting point of the glass and increase its brilliance.</note>. If one wishes to prepare this batch on a small scale, take 8 **Loth** <term>Loth</term> <note>A traditional German unit of weight; 1 Loth is approximately 15-17 grams (roughly half an ounce).</note> of crystal, 24 Loth of red lead, 32 **grains** <term>grains</term> <note>original: "Gran"; a small unit of weight, approximately 0.06 grams.</note> of right finely prepared **manganese** <term>manganese</term> <note>original: "Braunstein"; specifically manganese dioxide, used by glassmakers to create violet tones or to decolorize glass.</note>, and only 4 grains of powdered **zaffre** <term>zaffre</term> <note>original: "Zaffran"; though it literally means saffron, in the context of glassmaking and when paired with "blue smalt," it refers to "zaffre," a roasted cobalt ore used to produce blue colors.</note> or **blue smalt** <term>blue smalt</term> <note>original: "blaue Schmalta"; a pigment made of ground blue cobalt glass.</note>. Then mix these together, charge them into a **crucible** <term>crucible</term> <note>original: "Tiegel"; a ceramic container capable of withstanding the high heat of a furnace.</note>, and place it with other crucibles for melting.

### 64.
## To imitate the sapphire flux.

The **sapphire** is of a sky-blue color and is perfectly imitated through the **lunar sulfur** <term>lunar sulfur</term> <note>original: "lunarischen Sulphur"; in early modern chemistry, "lunar" (of the moon) usually related to silver, but here the author uses the term to describe the chemical essence or coloring principle found within cobalt ores.</note> that resides in **cobalt** <term>cobalt</term> <note>original: "Kobald"; a mineral long used to produce deep blue pigments in glass and ceramics.</note>, from which the **zaffre** <term>zaffre</term> <note>original: "Zaffra"; an impure form of cobalt oxide, often mixed with sand, used as a primary coloring agent for blue glass.</note> color is melted.

One takes one part of clear **flint** <term>flint</term> <note>original: "Kieselstein"; the source of silica, the primary ingredient in glass.</note> and likewise three parts of **red lead** <term>red lead</term> <note>original: "Mennige"; lead oxide, which acts as a flux to lower the melting point and increase the refractive index (sparkle) of the glass.</note>, and mixes this with just a little more zaffre or blue color. Some treat such materials with **nitric acid** <term>nitric acid</term> <note>original: "Scheidewasser"; literally "parting water," a strong acid used by chemists to separate metals or "pickle" minerals.</note> and believe it allows the material to be better handled; however, it is certain that such a blue color—which is itself nothing more than a glass previously prepared from flint and **calcined** <note>original: "calcinirten"; heated to a high temperature to remove volatile substances and reduce a material to powder.</note> cobalt—is very easily melted. It mixes in an instant with the other flint in the heat of the furnace, imparting its concentrated black-blue color to them and thereby becoming a quite beautiful sky-blue. And if one takes only a little zaffre for a large amount of flint, the color must become very light. Therefore, one can also imitate the **aquamarine** <term>aquamarine</term> <note>A pale blue or greenish-blue gemstone.</note> using these methods, when the zaffre is barely perceptible, as that stone only leans slightly toward the greenish-blue.

<summary>This page provides a recipe for creating "sapphire flux" (lead-glass imitation sapphire) using cobalt-based zaffre, flint, and red lead, while also explaining how to adjust the pigment to create imitation aquamarine.</summary>
<keywords>sapphire flux, cobalt, zaffre, red lead, flint, glassmaking, aquamarine, lunar sulfur, early chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for creating artificial gemstone fluxes. ### 65.
## To imitate the amethyst flux.

This is a color mixed together from blue and red, and it can be imitated very closely using **zaffre** <term>zaffre</term> <note>original: "Zaffran-Farbe"; while "Zaffran" usually means saffron (yellow), in historical glassmaking it frequently refers to "zaffre," the cobalt-blue pigment used to provide the blue base for the amethyst's purple hue.</note> with a bit of **manganese dioxide** <term>manganese dioxide</term> <note>original: "Braunstein"; literally "brown stone." In glassmaking, this mineral was used to provide violet tints or to clarify the glass batch.</note>. The **body** <note>original: "Corpus"; refers to the primary bulk material of the glass.</note> of all these **compositions** <note>original: "Composition"; the chemical mixture or recipe.</note> is the prepared **flint** <note>original: "Kieselstein"; the source of silica.</note>, and its primary **flux** <note>original: "Haupt-Fluß"; the substance added to lower the melting point of the silica.</note> is always **red lead** <note>original: "rothe Mennige"; lead oxide.</note>, along with prepared **saltpeter** <note>original: "Salpeter"; potassium nitrate.</note> or **salt of tartar** <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>original: "Weinstein-Salz"; potassium carbonate, which acts as a powerful alkaline flux.</note>, which must always be included as seen fit. Because the **salts** <note>original: "Salien"; refers to the chemical salts like saltpeter and tartar.</note> boil up high, the **crucible** <note>original: "Tiegel"; the heat-resistant ceramic pot used for melting glass.</note> must always remain one-quarter empty. 

The best method for this is: one part flint, three parts red lead, one part saltpeter, and one part **borax** or **salt of tartar** <note>original: "Sal Tartari"; another term for salt of tartar.</note>. If there were 20 pounds of flint, then 1 **lot** <term>lot</term> <note>original: "Loth"; a historical weight unit, approximately 15 to 18 grams.</note> of this blue zaffre color and 1 lot of powdered manganese dioxide can be enough to achieve an amethyst color. This mixture is then thoroughly melted together.

In the preceding sections, it will have been shown how to make the best **saffron of Mars** <term>saffron of Mars</term> <note>original: "Crocum Martis"; a chemical term for iron oxide, which produces yellow or red-brown tints in glass.</note> and **saffron of Venus** <term>saffron of Venus</term> <note>original: "Crocum Veneris"; a chemical term for copper oxide, used to produce green or turquoise colors.</note>, which one must look over and learn well, because these are needed here for...

### 67.
## To make the green emerald flux
->zu machen.<-

This gemstone takes its color from the **realm of Venus** <note>In alchemical tradition, the planet Venus is associated with the metal copper. Because copper produces green pigments like verdigris, the emerald is considered to belong to "Venus's realm."</note>, therefore, to imitate it, one must also treat it with the same; and all this is called working according to nature:

Take likewise 20 pounds of **flint**, 60 pounds of **red lead**, half a pound of good **distilled verdigris** <term>verdigris</term> <note>original: "Grünspan"; a green pigment formed by the reaction of acetic acid on copper plates.</note>, 6 pounds of melted **saltpeter** prepared in the manner described above, and 1 pound of **salt of tartar** <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>original: "Sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate, used here as a flux to lower the melting point.</note>; mix these well together, fill a large **melting crucible** up to one quarter <note>Filling only a quarter of the pot allows room for the mixture to foam and "boil" as the gases escape during the melting process without overflowing.</note>, and let it flow well.

### 68.
## To imitate a Turquoise.

This is a sky-blue and transparent gemstone <note>original: "Edelgestein"; although natural turquoise is opaque, 18th-century glass imitations or "fluxes" were often made to be transparent or translucent.</note> which is imitated in the following manner:

Take six parts of well-prepared **flint** <term>flint</term> <note>ground silica used as the base for glass.</note>, five parts of fine, clearly ground **mother-of-pearl**, two parts of **blue saffron-color** <note>original: "blaue Saffran-Farbe"; likely a metal oxide preparation, such as cobalt, processed in a manner similar to "saffron of Mars."</note>, one part **ultramarine** <note>A deep-blue pigment made from ground lapis lazuli.</note>, eight parts **saltpeter**, and sixteen parts **salt of tartar** <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>original: "Sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate, used as a flux to lower the melting point of the glass.</note> or **potash**. All of these are to be well mixed together in a **melting crucible**, prepared according to the previously described method, well **luted** <note>original: "verlutiret"; sealed with a clay paste to protect the contents from furnace gases.</note>, and then melted.

---

### 69.
## The Hyacinth Flux.

To one part of clearly ground **flint**, take three parts of **white lead** <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "Bleyweiß"; lead carbonate, used to increase the refractive index and brilliance of the glass.</note> and a small amount of **saffron of Mars** <term>saffron of Mars</term> <note>original: "Crocum Martis"; an iron oxide pigment that produces yellow, orange, or red tints.</note>. Mix these well and let them flow together. In this **composition-melting**, practical skill <note>original: "Handgriffe"; literally "hand-grips" or manual techniques.</note> must do the best work alongside experience, as one must learn all the advantages and techniques through working with the fire itself.

## To make a Diamond out of a Hyacinth.

The hyacinth <note>A reddish-orange gemstone, usually a variety of zircon or garnet.</note> should be placed in a piece of hammered **gold leaf**, with some **flowers of sulfur** <term>flowers of sulfur</term> <note>original: "Schwefel-Blumen"; a fine powder of sulfur produced through sublimation, used here for its chemical reactivity when heated.</note> added to it; this is to be wrapped and beaten tightly all around, then placed into a small **steel box**, and this box should be set in a small **melting crucible** in a **blast furnace** <term>blast furnace</term> <note>original: "Wind-Ofen"; a furnace designed to reach high temperatures through a strong draft of air.</note>. There it must remain for four hours so that the gold in the box flows against the hyacinth, and afterwards it must be allowed to cool gradually <note>original: "successivè". This process describes a heat-treatment method intended to "decolorize" a colored gemstone to make it resemble a diamond.</note>.

->71.<-

## To imitate very beautiful Glossy Pearls.

For these pearls, the finest **mother-of-pearl** must be pounded into a delicate powder, then finely sifted, and half as much beautiful white **barley flour** added to it, and moistened with **May dew** <note>original: "Mayenthau"; dew collected in the month of May was highly prized in alchemy and early chemistry for its perceived purity and unique properties.</note> (which must first be well-aged <note>original: "putrificiret"; though literally "putrefied," in this context it refers to the alchemical process of allowing a substance to digest or ferment until it clarifies.</note>), and thus made into a dough. From this dough, one now forms pearls in whatever size is desired, and sticks a **hog's bristle** into them so that they get a hole, and allows them to dry well on the bristle. Or, one must take fine **snail shells**, these [with] <meta>catchword: mit</meta>

Afterwards, they must be rubbed with white **bran** and **salt** and dried gently.

### 72.

## Representing the four elements of the earth—fire, water, air, and earth—in a single bottle.

For this purpose, **black enamel** <note>original: "schwarzer Schmelz"; likely a type of dark glass or vitreous paste used in jewelry and crafts.</note> can be most suitably used, which should be crushed somewhat moderately, though not entirely into a fine flour-like powder; this ensures that, when moved, it always settles back to the bottom quickly. This is intended to represent the **Earth**. For the **Water**, one can most conveniently use a <term>liquor of tartar</term> <note>original: "Liquor Tartari"; a saturated solution of potassium carbonate.</note>, or <term>oil of tartar by deliquescence</term> <note>original: "Oleum Tartari per deliquium"; a chemical solution formed when potassium carbonate absorbs enough atmospheric moisture to dissolve.</note>, and mix such a liquid with some **ultramarine** or **lapis lazuli** (if one can obtain it) to represent the color of the sea. For the element of **Air**, one uses the best <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Vini"; high-proof distilled alcohol.</note> that can be found. For the element of **Fire**, it is best to take a well-clarified **turpentine oil**, which is prepared as follows:

One distills the **turpentine** in a glass flask, either in a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "Balneo"; specifically "Balneum Mariae," a gentle heating method using boiling water.</note> or in ashes <note>original: "Aschen"; a sand or ash bath used in early chemistry to provide steady, high heat.</note>, so that the water and oil pass over together, being quite pure and transparent. The oil will then float on top, which is removed with a funnel; to represent **Fire**, it can be given a bright yellow color, such as with **saffron**; (if this oil with **bugloss** <note>original: "Ochsen-"; the catchword "Zun" indicates "Ochsenzunge" (Alkanet/Bugloss), a plant root used to dye oils red or yellow.</note>

### 74.
### A Metal Cement for Glass,
### Stone, Brass, Iron and
### Gemstones.

For such a cement, one takes one part **turpentine** and one part **mastic** <note>original: "Mastix"; a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used historically in varnishes and adhesives.</note>, refined for gemstones; melt these in a glass bowl, add some small, filed **glue** <note>Likely animal hide glue that has been shredded or filed into small pieces to facilitate melting.</note> into it, and sprinkle it with a brush, and then it is ready. One places **isinglass** <note>original: "Hausen-Blasen"; a high-quality collagen/glue made from the dried swim bladders of fish, particularly sturgeon.</note> into water, letting it soak therein for several days, then boils it down in a small copper dish until a glue remains at the bottom, which is used for such cement.

->76.<-

## To make a good green ink.

The most common way to make green ink is to take good **verdigris** <note>original: "Grünspan"; a green pigment historically created by exposing copper plates to fermenting grape skins or vinegar.</note>, dissolve it in wine vinegar, let it stand in the warmth for several weeks, and add some **alum** <note>original: "Alaun"; a mineral salt used as a mordant to help the color "fix" to the paper.</note>, as well as a little **gum arabic** <note>original: "Gummi Arabicum"; a hardened sap from the acacia tree used to bind pigments and provide a glossy finish.</note> to the mixture, so that the green ink may be enhanced with a shine. One can also, in summer and especially in the springtime, take green **elderberry leaves**, crush them, press out their juice, and mix some alum into it; this also produces a beautiful green ink of a somewhat dark green color, with which one can write and dye.

### 79.

## To make a water that dispels all spots from the face.

In the summer, take fresh broad bean husks <note>original: "Bohnen-Vaselen"; the outer pods of the bean plant.</note>, crush them, and rub them well in a bowl; one pound of white bread crumbs; and one fresh green peeled gourd <note>original: "Kürbis"; likely a pumpkin or squash.</note>. Let the whole mixture cook together in a large glass flask <note>original: "Glas-Kolben"; a vessel used for distillation or slow infusion.</note> for a day and night, then press it out well and save it for use. ## 80.

# To give iron great hardness, and as it were to make it into steel.

This is a reliable and fine masterpiece <note>original: "Kunst-stück"; a term used in early modern texts for a clever technique, trade secret, or specialized craft process.</note> that many have held to be a great secret <note>original: "Arcanum"; a Latin term used by alchemists and early scientists to denote a profound mystery of nature or a powerful chemical preparation.</note>, and much money has been earned through it. Whether the best English or Swedish steel <note>At this time, Sweden and England were renowned for producing the highest quality steel, essential for tools and weaponry.</note> can be made from every kind of iron by this method, I will not exactly say. I only admire the fine hardness that is given to the metal, allowing many useful things to be manufactured from it; and if it then serves its purpose, one can be satisfied, whether it is or appears perfectly similar to steel or not. The world often dwells more on verbal disputes regarding a thing than on the thing itself, which a rational person alone should focus on; one should not be concerned whether a thing is called Italian, French, or English, but rather look to see if the thing provides its required utility; then one can

one might well allow such names to stand. Foreign names often sell a thing more readily than if it were called by a German name, though a deception often lies behind it.

For this iron-work, take small iron rods, about an <term>ell</term> <note>A traditional unit of measurement; in German regions, it usually measured between 55 and 85 centimeters.</note> long. Construct a <term>calcining furnace</term> <note>A furnace used to heat materials to high temperatures to change their physical or chemical properties without melting them, often used in early chemistry to "purify" or harden substances.</note> for this purpose, place the rods inside, and <term>stratify</term> <note>original: "stratificire"; to arrange in alternating layers. Here, the iron is packed between layers of a carbon-rich powder.</note> them with a powder of beech charcoal dust, oven soot, and filed cow or ox horns. Then, maintain a brisk <term>calcining fire</term> for 24 hours, so that the iron might glow with only a dull, <term>black heat</term> <note>A stage of heating metal where it is hot enough to undergo chemical changes but has not yet reached a bright red or white glow.</note>. Afterward, let it cool, remove it, and test whether the iron is hard enough for its intended use; for the benefit of this process will then surely be revealed.

### 81.
# To transform iron into the best copper.

That this is a certain and proven matter, I wish to testify here before everyone. And although in this small trial that I intend to present here, no great benefit or profit is produced, but rather, by all appearances, it results more in a <term>curiosity</term> <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, a "curiosity" referred to a scientific marvel or a demonstration of nature’s hidden powers, often prized for its wonder rather than its utility.</note>, I nonetheless wish to show those who hold natural curiosities to be of no pleasure, but rather [expect] from all things,

things they wish to undertake must yield an immediate profit, I have recommended this little "play" <note>original: "Lustspiel"; here used metaphorically to mean a demonstration or experiment performed for intellectual pleasure or curiosity.</note> for further reflection, as more lies behind it than many imagine, and a rational person will be able to grasp it with their thoughts.

Take blue <term>Cyprian vitriol</term> <note>Copper sulfate, named for Cyprus, a historic source of copper.</note>, dissolve 2 <term>Loth</term> <note>A German unit of weight; one Loth is approximately 14.6 to 17 grams depending on the region.</note> of it in fresh water, and add to it one Loth of finely filed iron. In an instant, the iron will transform into red copper. Dry this solution or boil it down until dry, then place it into a small <term>crucible</term> and let it melt together with some <term>potash</term> <note>A potassium-rich salt obtained from wood ashes, used as a flux to help metals melt and to remove impurities.</note> before the bellows. You will then find a beautiful, malleable copper; however, it is too expensive for profit or sale, for the Cyprian vitriol costs 12 <term>Groschen</term> <note>A silver coin used in various German states.</note> per pound, and no other vitriol will work for this, whether it be Hungarian or Salzburgian vitriol. Ponder this well.

### 85.

## To melt <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurium"; also known as quicksilver.</note> in order to make a white copper from it.

For this work, one has a large copper sphere made, which can be joined together in the middle like a <term>canister</term> <note>original: "Büchsen"; a cylindrical container or box.</note> and closes tightly; then let <term>living mercury</term> <note>original: "lebendigen Mercurium"; liquid elemental mercury.</note> be rubbed down with <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arsenico"</note> so that the mercury disappears completely within it and becomes invisible. Then, place such material into the copper canister, <term>lute</term> <note>original: "verlutirt"; to seal a vessel with an airtight clay or paste to prevent the escape of vapors.</note> it well, and place it in a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "Sand-Capellen"; a container of sand used to provide even, indirect heat.</note>. Apply fire beneath it successively, and finally, increase the heat so that the sphere may glow at the bottom; thus the mercury melts together with the arsenic and attaches itself to the copper sphere, at which point one has fired it enough. Now, let the fire go out and break open the sphere; there will be found a silver-colored, brittle material. This is [melted] together with the copper sphere, and if the result is not yet fluid and malleable enough, more copper must be melted into it. Thus one obtains a beautiful white copper, useful for making all kinds of vessels. However, because of the arsenic, it is difficult and dangerous to grind <note>original: "reiben"; likely referring to the processing of the toxic arsenic and mercury mixture, which releases dangerous dust or fumes.</note>,

### 86.
## To make the most beautiful white potter's glaze, for beautiful white tiles and stove moldings.

The proper white glaze, which is used for beautiful white <term>stove moldings</term> <note>original: "Ofen-Leisten"; decorative ceramic strips or borders used in the construction of tiled masonry stoves.</note> and other fine pottery work, is not yet known to every potter: for it does not consist solely of <term>silver litharge</term> <note>original: "Silber-Glötte"; lead monoxide, often a byproduct of silver refining, used as a flux in glass and glazes.</note>, like other common pot glazes; rather, it must first be prepared from the metals <term>lead</term> <note>original: "Bley"</note> and <term>tin</term> <note>original: "Zinn"</note>. Those masters who know this keep it as a secret of their profession, and do not let every <term>journeyman</term> <note>original: "Gesellen"; a worker who has completed an apprenticeship but is not yet a master.</note> see it, nor do they give it to their apprentices upon completion of their training.

Because this glaze depends entirely upon a beautiful white color which must not lean toward yellow, tin must be added to the lead during its preparation, which I wish to teach and make known to everyone here.

One takes 16 pounds of <term>lead</term> <note>original: "Bley"</note> for a batch and prepares a special <term>calcining furnace</term> <note>original: "Calcinir-Ofen"; a furnace used to heat substances to high temperatures in the presence of air to cause oxidation.</note> for this purpose, where 4 to 6 roof tiles are laid tightly together at the bottom, and the joints are sealed with good <term>lute</term> <note>original: "Luto"; a clay-based sealant or cement used to make joints airtight and heat-resistant.</note>, so that the molten lead does not leak through. Beneath these roof tiles comes the fire, which must make this roof-tile hearth glowing hot with its heat; into this, the aforementioned lead is now placed, and when it is melted, it is stirred back and forth constantly with a small iron rake until it has been burned into <term>ash</term> <note>original: "Asche"; here referring to the metallic oxide formed by heating the metal in air.</note>, which is achieved through diligent stirring. 

When almost all of the lead has been turned into ash in this way, one adds 5 pounds of <term>tin</term> <note>original: "Zinn"</note> to it and stirs them together until the tin also becomes an ash, which occurs alongside the lead ash within a few hours. Then, when everything has turned to ash and no more flowing metal remains within it, the fire in the furnace is allowed to go out, and the <term>calcined</term> <note>original: "calcinirte"; oxidized through heating.</note> ash is removed.

The potters take one bowl of such metallic ash and five bowls of fine, washed <term>gravel-sand</term> <note>original: "Kieß-Sand"; likely a high-silica sand used for glass making.</note>, and place them into large pots or <term>melting crucibles</term> <note>original: "Schmelz-Tiegel"</note>, covered at the top with salt a finger's breadth high. They melt these together, and thus a white <term>slag</term> <note>original: "Schlacke"; in this context, it refers to a "frit" or a pre-glass material that will be ground down later.</note> or stone is formed, which is quite shiny,

...will be the most beautiful glaze. For with lead alone, or its <term>litharge</term> <note>original: "Glötte"; lead monoxide, a yellow mineral used as a flux in glassmaking</note>, such a glaze would never achieve such a fine and beautiful whiteness, which makes the <term>stove tiles</term> <note>original: "Kacheln"; the decorative ceramic tiles used to face large masonry heaters common in Central European homes</note>, in particular, appear beautiful and elegant. In contrast, if only lead litharge, or even <term>lead ash</term> <note>original: "Bley-Asche"</note> and sand, are used for such a glaze without tin, it will only turn yellow, but not white. Yet the success of the work depends on this, as it is the most important <term>secret of the trade</term> <note>original: "Handgriffe"; literally "hand-moves" or professional techniques</note>; therefore, I have correctly described all of this here.

### 87.

# The most beautiful blue glaze for potter’s work, useful for jugs, bowls, and plates, as well as for porcelain painting.

For this blue color, the ordinary <term>blue starch</term> <note>original: "blaue Stärcke"; here referring to "laundry bluing" or ground smalt used to whiten linens or as a pigment</note> or blue color works very well, as it is by nature a very fine, subtle blue glass or <term>slag</term> <note>original: "Schlacken"; the glass-like byproduct of smelting</note>, and is exactly the kind of material that potters and <term>stove-makers</term> <note>original: "Häfner"; specifically a potter who specializes in making vessels and tiles for masonry heaters</note> seek to create for their glaze. However, if they have to buy it as <term>blue smalt</term> <note>original: "blaue Smalta"; a pigment made by grinding cobalt glass into a fine powder</note>, it proves too expensive in many places. Nevertheless, in case of necessity—if one knows no other way and has fine plates, jugs, or <term>stove tiles</term> <note>original: "Kacheln"</note> to paint that will recoup the cost—this blue color is the best for the task, as it is already prepared and ready to use. One only needs to melt it with four parts of beautiful, pure, and well-<term>calcined pebbles</term> <note>original: "calcinirten Kiesel"; flint or quartz stones heated and quenched to make them easy to grind into silica powder</note>, mixed with one part <term>lead litharge</term> <note>original: "Bley-Glötte"; lead monoxide, used as a flux to lower the melting point of the glass</note>; in this way, one will obtain a glaze-glass that becomes a very deep blue. 

If, however, one can obtain <term>blue color-cobalt</term> <note>original: "Farben-Kobald"</note> or <term>bismuth</term> <note>original: "Wißmuth"; bismuth and cobalt were often found in the same ores in German mines, and early chemists often confused their properties in pigment production</note> in a certain place, which yields a blue color, one may prepare such a blue glaze oneself without great expense, namely in the following manner:

One roasts and <term>calcines</term> <note>the process of heating a substance to a high temperature but below its melting point to cause chemical change or physical dissociation</note> these cobalt or bismuth ores in an open <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>original: "Reverberir-Ofen"; a furnace that reflects heat from the roof onto the material, preventing direct contact with the fuel</note> so that all arsenical smoke and vapor is removed. Otherwise, they look exactly like the blue-pigment cobalt. ...[from] the Harz in Saxony, Bohemia, Silesia, Hesse, and other places besides, one can first make a test of such cobalt, so that one may see how much sand or clear <term>flint</term> <note>original: "Kiesel"; typically referring to quartz pebbles or high-silica stones used in glassmaking</note> one actually needs for a full <term>batch</term> <note>original: "Versatz"; the specific ratio of raw materials prepared for the furnace</note>.

By adjusting the proportions accordingly, one can then make the color of the glaze as deep or as light as desired.

### 90.
## To Imitate the Black Glaze.

The black glaze, which is used especially for beautiful black stoves <note>Reference to the "Kachelofen," the traditional ceramic tile stoves used for home heating in Central Europe</note>, is no less a popular color. It is prepared from **manganese** <note>original: "Braunstein"; literally "brown stone," referring to manganese dioxide, which creates a deep black or purple-black when concentrated</note> as an additive to the sand, namely:
Six bowls of sand or ground pebbles, including 2 bowls of **lead litharge**, 2 bowls of powdered manganese, one bowl of saltpeter, and some potash. Mix these well together and let them flow together in a strong fire, being well **luted** <note>original: "verlutiret"; sealed with a paste or clay to prevent the escape of gases or entry of air</note>. Then, it is ground fine and clear on a large **grinding stone** or a special machine, and thereafter used for the glaze.

The

At the **Fichtel Mountain** <note>original: "Fichtel-Berge"; a mountain range in Upper Franconia, Bavaria, known for its rich mining and glassmaking history</note>, behind the village of **Bischofsgrün** (on the Bayreuth side), there lie many large stones which look not unlike **sandstones** and are interspersed with black nests as large as peas and pinheads. When melted, these yield an entirely coal-black glass; this then provides a good black **glaze** <note>original: "Glasur"</note>, which is used there on **porcelain** <note>original: "Porcelain"; in this period, this often referred to fine white earthenware or "half-porcelain" common in the region before the widespread success of hard-paste porcelain</note> and other fine pottery and glasswork. Therefore, a potter who wishes to skillfully imitate beautiful glazes must diligently examine the minerals and rocks in the mountains, and industriously test them for glazes with **litharge** <note>original: "Glötte"; lead monoxide used as a flux to lower the melting point</note> and **potash** <note>original: "Pottaschen"</note>, as well as **saltpeter** <note>original: "Salpeter"</note>, and take note of where they came from. He will certainly encounter many curious glazes that no one can easily imitate, as mineral rocks and **clays** <note>original: "Letten"; specifically fat, loamy clay or marl</note> often produce such a beautiful glass color that no one would ever invent it, even if they spent many years experimenting with artifice. However, he must know and remember from which place it came; for he will not be able to replicate it from another pit <note>original: "Loch"; literally "hole," referring to a small excavation or mineral source</note>, because the rocks and minerals have very peculiar properties relative to one another. I have often found such wondrous glazes through testing and investigation that I myself had to marvel at them. And this is the right way to produce something truly masterful.

### § I.
## The Art of Making Metals Grow.

This is a secret work of natural art, and there is more behind its operation than one might imagine. A naturalist <note>original: "Naturverständiger"; literally one who understands nature</note> can quite easily see and recognize the foundation and growth of the metallic essence from this, and apply it to extraordinary use.

One should **calcine** <term>calcine</term> <note>original: "calcinire"; to heat a mineral to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances and make it brittle enough to powder</note> beautiful white pebbles, especially those found in rivers, three to six times; quench them in water every time so that they become brittle and fall apart. Pulverize them until they are completely clear and fine, pass them through a hair-sieve, and mix them with 3 parts of good **Salt of Tartar** <term>Salt of Tartar</term> <note>original: "Sal Tartari" or "Weinstein-Saltz"; potassium carbonate, a powerful flux used to lower the melting point of minerals</note>, which shall be taught hereafter, along with how to make its **Oil** <note>original: "Oleo"; Oil of Tartar, a concentrated liquid form of the salt used in chemical reactions</note> in a very special way. Now, carry this mixed mass into a glowing **crucible** <note>original: "Schmelz-Tiegel"</note> in a good **draft furnace** <note>original: "Wind-Ofen"; a furnace designed to reach high temperatures by drawing in a strong current of air</note>, and always let it melt well, a few spoonfuls at a time, adding one after the other until it is all gone. When it melts well together, stir it thoroughly with a red-hot iron; and once it has melted well for an hour, lift the crucible out and let it cool—

## 92.

# A special secret alchemical operation to prepare a **liquor** <term>liquor</term> from **antimony** <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "ex Antimonio"; antimony was a vital metal in alchemy, often associated with the "Grey Wolf" that "devours" other metals during purification.</note> that does not evaporate in fire, but remains a liquid; yet in the cold, it crystallizes into an astral salt.

One takes 2 to 3 pounds of potash, or deflagrated saltpeter <note>Potash and saltpeter are alkaline salts used as fluxes to lower the melting point of minerals.</note>, allows it to melt in a crucible by the fire in a good blast furnace, then **gradually** <term>gradually</term> <note>original: "successivè"</note> adds 2 to 3 pounds of powdered **antimony** <term>antimony</term> into it, lets these melt together, and afterwards lifts it from the fire, or lets the fire go out, and breaks the crucible in two. Thus one will find a brown **mass** <term>mass</term> <note>original: "Massa"</note>; this is crushed small in a mortar, and one pours cold water onto such **material** <term>material</term> <note>original: "Materia"</note> and allows it to dissolve whatever will do so of its own accord. It is then poured off into a large, wide sugar-glass or flask, and **precipitated** <term>precipitated</term> <note>original: "præcipitiret"; to cause a solid to settle out of a solution.</note> with **aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Aquafort"; literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</note>. Thus a thick red-brown **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Schwefel"; here referring to "Sulfur of Antimony" (antimony trisulfide), prized by alchemists for its deep red color.</note> falls; this is dried, finely sifted, and distilled through a glass retort. Thus a strong vapor and drops pass through; these are allowed to go into a small receiver and are saved. When one has enough of these, one puts these **spirits** <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "Spiritus"; the volatile, "spiritual" essence of a substance captured through distillation.</note> into a glass retort and distills the moisture or wateriness away from it, or even lets the same in a glass

The resulting "liquor" is notable for its resistance to heat and its ability to crystallize into "astral salt" when cooled.</summary>

### 93.

## To prepare a fragrant **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur"; in this context, sulfur refers to the essential "soul" or oily, flammable principle of a substance rather than just the chemical element.</note> from **antimony** <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonio"</note> that smells like the finest **ambergris** <term>ambergris</term> <note>original: "Ambra"; a rare, sweet-smelling substance used in high-end perfumes.</note>.

This experiment serves as proof that metals and **minerals** <term>minerals</term> <note>original: "Mineralia"</note> are fragrant in their innermost essence: When **antimony** <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonium"</note> is distilled in the manner previously described, a very subtle **sublimate** <term>sublimate</term> <note>a solid substance produced by heating a material until it becomes a vapor and then cooling it back into solid form.</note> or **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> comes off, though there is very little of it; however, when it is gathered together, it smells like the finest **ambergris** <term>ambergris</term> <note>original: "Ambra"</note>. An experienced **chemist** <term>chemist</term> <note>original: "Chymicus"</note> will know how to further use this **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur"</note>, as I do not wish to boast or brag about all these experiments. Whatever anyone lacks, they will surely know how to seek for themselves.

## 94.

### To make a ruby-red glass from **antimony** <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonio"</note> that is **stable** <note>original: "fix"; in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand the heat of a furnace without vaporizing or escaping as smoke.</note>, and which **coagulates** <note>solidifies or hardens a liquid metal.</note> one part of **mercury** <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurium"</note> to one hundred parts, so that it allows itself to be melted and does not evaporate; it also **tinctures** <note>To "tincture" means to change the color or internal nature of a metal, often the first step in supposed transmutation.</note> copper white, though it is very brittle when cast.

I describe this experiment not for the sake of the great profit I have drawn from it, but rather because I found it so, and for the sake of further reflection: for I have seen from it that something extraordinary is hidden behind it, whether or not I had already correctly achieved and made the same.

### 95.

## To make a precious **salt**, with which I once, in a small test, transformed one part to 100 parts of **copper** into living blue **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium".</note>, which could be boiled in **nitric acid** <term>nitric acid</term> <note>original: "Scheidewasser," literally "parting water." It was used by alchemists to separate (part) gold from silver; it typically dissolves copper and mercury easily.</note> for three hours and would not **dissolve** <note>original: "solvirt".</note>.

This is a very rare and secret masterpiece, which I performed only once and, due to time and particular circumstances, have not **repeated** <note>original: "repetiret".</note> since. It did not immediately produce the **effect** <note>original: "Effect".</note> that such a salt was intended to perform; however, when I tested it on copper, I obtained this mercury from it. The **process** <note>original: "Proceß".</note> for it was as follows:

I took one pound of **orpiment** <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "Auri pigmentum"; a deep-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral. I pulverized this, mixed in another three pounds of clearly crushed and finely sifted **orpiment**, placed it into a **sublimation vessel** <note>original: "Sublimir-Gefäß".</note>, and **sublimed** <note>original: "sublimirte"; the chemical process where a solid turns directly into a gas when heated and then reverts to a solid upon cooling.</note> it over an open fire for 24 hours continuously, until all the **sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur".</note> had risen into the **aludels** <term>aludels</term> <note>original: "Aludeln"; earthenware pots or pear-shaped vessels used as condensers in the sublimation process, often stacked in a long line or "chimney."</note>. Then I let the fire go out, so—

### 96.
# To bring Silver into a soft, liquid mass, like Turpentine.

This chemical experiment has been kept as a secret by many; therefore, I wish to include it here as well, for whoever may find it useful. The process is as follows:

One takes 1 **Lot** <note>A historical unit of weight, approximately 1/2 ounce or 15 grams.</note> of **horn silver** <term>horn silver</term> <note>original: "Luna Cornua"; silver chloride, which has a horn-like appearance when fused.</note> (which is a **silver calx** <note>original: "Silber-Kalck"; a metallic residue or powder produced by heating.</note> dissolved in **parting water** <term>parting water</term> <note>original: "Scheide-Wasser"; nitric acid, used to separate silver from gold.</note>, precipitated with common salt, and thoroughly rinsed with warm water). Mix into this 2 **Lots** of white powdered **arsenic** <note>original: "Arsenicum"</note>, place it in a small glass **retort** <term>retort</term> <note>A glassware vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances.</note>, and pour 5 **Lots** of red **oil of vitriol** <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleum Vitrioli"; concentrated sulfuric acid.</note> over it. Place this retort in a small **sand bath** <note>original: "Sand-Capelle"; a container of sand heated by a furnace to provide even, indirect heat.</note>, set a small **receiver** <note>original: "Vorlage"; a vessel used to collect the condensed vapors from the distillation.</note> before it, and distill gently. By this means, the silver dissolves with the arsenic, and the excess oil of vitriol passes over into the small receiver. When everything volatile has passed over, allow the fire to go out. When it has cooled, take out the retort and break it in two; there you will find a yellow, transparent **mass** <note>original: "Massa"</note> similar to white **turpentine** <note>original: "Terpentin"; here referring to a thick, resinous consistency.</note>, which can be cut and pressed, and which flows again when heated.

<summary>This page describes a specific alchemical procedure to transform silver into a soft, resin-like consistency using arsenic and sulfuric acid. This "secret" process results in a malleable, translucent yellow substance that melts when heated.</summary>

### 97.

# To prepare common Mercury or Quicksilver into an Oil.

One takes 1 pound of **liquid mercury** <term>liquid mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurium vivum"; elemental mercury in its fluid state.</note>, and dissolves it little by little in 3 pounds of good **strong water** <term>strong water</term> <note>original: "Aquafort" (Aqua Fortis); nitric acid.</note>; however, this dissolution must occur in a **flask** <term>flask</term> <note>original: "Kolben"; a glass vessel with a round body and a neck.</note> within a **sand bath** <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "Sand-Capelle"; a container of sand heated by a furnace to provide indirect, even heat.</note>, so that the **parting water** <term>parting water</term> <note>original: "Scheide-Wasser"; another name for nitric acid, used to separate metals.</note> can boil upon it. When everything has been dissolved one after the other, the solutions are poured together, and in a **flask** (upon which an **alembic** <term>alembic</term> <note>original: "Helm"; the cap of the distilling apparatus where vapors condense.</note> and a **receiver** <term>receiver</term> <note>original: "Vorlage"; the vessel used to collect the distillate.</note> are **sealed with lute** <term>sealed with lute</term> <note>original: "lutiret"; joints were sealed with a paste or "lute" to prevent vapors from escaping.</note>) the **parting water** or **strong water** is drawn off (distilled) again until dry. 

When this has been done, the precipitated **mercury** can be divided into two parts; one part is dissolved and resolved with water, the other however with wine vinegar. In this way, one obtains two kinds of **oils** <term>oils</term> <note>original: "Olea"; in early chemistry, this referred to a concentrated, viscous liquid rather than a fatty oil.</note> from it, a red one and a white one. When these have dissolved in both the vinegar and the water, one filters them through blotting paper, lets the pure solutions boil down again, and then repeats this dissolution—one with water and the other with vinegar. Let it be filtered and purified again, and then boil it down until it begins to **crystallize** <term>crystallize</term> <note>original: "Anschiessen"; the point where crystals begin to form in a saturated solution.</note>, or better yet, let it **evaporate** in glass bowls. Thus, in the vinegar solution, long four-sided **crystals** form, similar to **saltpeter** <term>saltpeter</term> <note>original: "Salpeter"; potassium nitrate.</note>, but yellow; these

### 98.

## To prepare arsenic such that it opens up metals and minerals in the fire.

Arsenic possesses a very subtle and penetrating quality; it readily enters into metals and attacks them, though it achieves little of permanence with them. However, when it is **fixed** <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "fixiret". In alchemy, "fixing" refers to the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or escapes when heated.</note> by **saltpeter** <term>saltpeter</term> <note>potassium nitrate, a powerful oxidizing agent.</note>, it becomes a **key to the metallic kingdom** and dissolves metals during smelting. Let no one think here that I am committing the folly of attempting to prepare the so-called <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> thereby; rather, I am only demonstrating its utility within the mineral kingdom, from which it originates and to which it is to be returned.

Take 1 pound of arsenic, pulverize it, and also take an equal amount of saltpeter. Mix these well together and place them in a crucible (which must only be half-full) within a good, well-closed **wind-furnace** <term>wind-furnace</term> <note>original: "Wind-Ofen"; a furnace designed to create a natural draft of air to reach high temperatures without the use of bellows.</note>. Or, if the furnace otherwise has a good draft, let these melt together. The **mass** <note>original: "Massa".</note> will rise to the top of the crucible and fill it entirely until the volatility of the spirits has passed and the saltpeter has been thoroughly **alkalized** <term>alkalized</term> <note>original: "alcalisiret"; the process where the saltpeter reacts with the arsenic to form a more stable, alkaline salt.</note> by the arsenic; then it falls

...together in the crucible, it flows properly, albeit with great smoke, and no longer rises up <note>The author is describing the mixture settling after its initial volatile reaction.</note>. This flux must be well protected from the air; otherwise, it **liquefies** in the air <note>This refers to deliquescence, where a substance absorbs moisture from the atmosphere until it dissolves.</note>. Take 3 parts of this **fixed arsenic** <note>original: "figirten Arsenico"</note>, 1 part of **Saffron of Mars** <term>Saffron of Mars</term> <note>original: "Crocus Martis"; a term for iron oxide (rust) prepared in a specific way.</note>, or **Saffron of Venus** <term>Saffron of Venus</term> <note>original: "Crocus Veneris"; a term for copper oxide.</note>, or **Sulfur of Antimony** <term>Sulfur of Antimony</term> <note>original: "Sulphur Antimonii"; likely antimony trisulfide.</note>, and melt them together in a crucible. Then pour it out or break the melting crucible and place the material in a glass bowl exposed to the air; it flows into a **red oil** <note>original: "rothen Oleum"</note>, and other such chemical manipulations.

## To distill the red oil from lead.

The processes for working with lead are cited in many chemical books, explaining how a **red oil** <note>original: "rothes Oleum"</note> is to be prepared from it, which is said to **coagulate** <term>coagulate</term> <note>In alchemy, to "coagulate" mercury was to turn the liquid metal into a solid, a key step in creating silver or gold.</note> mercury and turn it into silver or gold. During this operation, through an oversight—as I was performing this for the first time—the retort shattered. If every **natural philosopher** <term>natural philosopher</term> <note>original: "Naturforscher"; an early scientist or alchemist studying the natural world.</note> would write down such natural investigations and reveal the practical techniques to the world, we would soon gain a clearer light regarding the **secrets of nature** <note>original: "Geheimnissen der Natur".</note>. However, envy prevents most from doing so, and many lack the opportunity to test everything.

### 100.
## How to prepare a living mercury from common salt.
<note>original: "Aus dem Sale communi einen lebendigen Mercurium zuzubereiten." In alchemy, "living mercury" often refers to a purified or "quickened" form of the metal, or a specific solvent sought for the Great Work.</note>

Here, I must describe a curious work regarding **common salt** <term>common salt</term> <note>original: "Sale communi"; sodium chloride.</note> which I discovered by chance <note>original: "par hazard"</note> when I undertook to investigate it.

I dissolved one **measure** <term>measure</term> <note>original: "Metze"; a historical dry volume measure, roughly equivalent to a peck or several liters depending on the region.</note> of **Halle salt** <term>Halle salt</term> <note>original: "Hällisches Salz"; salt harvested from the famous brine springs of Halle, Germany.</note> in rain-water, and filtered the **solution** <term>solution</term> <note>original: "Solution"</note> well; then I let it boil very gently in a good and fire-resistant pot. When it had boiled down to half, I repeatedly poured in more hot water, which I kept sitting by the fire for this very reason. This boiling I performed continuously for 3 whole days and nights, one after the other; for I knew from experience that through this, my salt would eventually become entirely **diaphanous** <term>diaphanous</term> <note>original: "diaphan"; transparent or translucent.</note> or transparent, and I wanted to **anatomize** <term>anatomize</term> <note>original: "anatomiren"; in this context, to chemically decompose or break down a substance to its core components.</note> this transparent salt-body once again.

As the third night was nearly over, I let my salt boil down completely, so intensely that no moisture remained at all, whereupon my pot finally shattered. Upon cooling, I had a very large piece of transparent salt, just like a **crystal stone** <term>crystal stone</term> <note>original: "Crystallen-Stein".</note>. This salt I crushed coarsely, dissolved it again in rain-water, filtered it, and boiled it quite suddenly—and not as I had done the first time—in clear **bubbles** <term>bubbles</term> <note>original: "Blasen"; this may refer to the physical bubbles of a rolling boil or the "Blase," a specific type of copper boiling vessel used in distillation.</note>, and so this salt separated into two

After a while, I wanted to check my crucible and gripped it with a pair of tongs; there I saw a brown **mass** <term>mass</term> <note>original: "Massa"</note> on top. And this is what I wished to report concerning this matter.

Once, I took 4 pounds of crushed **Antimony** <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonium"; likely stibnite, the most common ore of antimony, which was a central material in the search for the "Philosopher's Mercury."</note>, poured distilled rain-water over it, and boiled it likewise for 3 whole days and nights consecutively in a good pot. ### 104.

## Two Inextinguishable Lights by the Lord Abbot Trithemius of Sponheim.

These two manuscripts concerning these lights were copied by a disciple of the Lord Abbot from his own manuscript, and from him in turn by Mr. Korndörffer <note>Bartholomäus Korndörffer was a 16th-century figure often associated with alchemical and Rosicrucian writings.</note>. We would have received no report or knowledge of them if the Lord Abbot had not presented a light of this kind to a great potentate. This Abbot bore the name Trithemius Magnus <note>Latin: "Trithemius the Great." Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a celebrated Benedictine abbot, lexicographer, and chronicler, though he was also famously accused of practicing magic and alchemy.</note>, lived in the time of Emperor Maximilian I <note>Reigned 1508–1519; known as the "Last Knight," he was a great patron of the arts and sciences, including the more "hermetic" pursuits.</note>, and was one of the most skillful and experienced men. Once, he set up a light prepared in the manner described below for Emperor Maximilian in one of the imperial rooms, but he placed it in such a way that one perceived only its glow. Some time afterward, a great mortality <note>A historical term for a plague or epidemic.</note> occurred in the imperial capital city; the Emperor saw himself forced because of this to go elsewhere, and did not return there for twenty years. At last, the Emperor traveled back to that place.

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on zinc oxide's chemical affinities before introducing a legendary account of Abbot Johannes Trithemius's "everlasting lamps" provided to Emperor Maximilian I.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the anecdote about Emperor Maximilian I returning to his residence after a twenty-year absence to find the "everlasting light" installed by Johannes Trithemius still burning. It transitions from this legendary account into practical alchemical instructions for creating such a lamp.</meta>

...the Lord Abbot Trithemius had long since passed away. When the Emperor arrived at the residence, he thought of the light presented to him by the Abbot, for which he had ordered 6,000 crowns to be paid, and went immediately to the place to inspect it. He was struck with astonishment when he found it in the chamber, just as it had been set up by the Lord Abbot, still unextinguished. Those who had remained at the castle collectively claimed to have constantly perceived a certain glow from this light, similar to the light of a sanctuary lamp in churches. The Emperor left this light there undisturbed, and some say: it burns even to this day.

This is certainly a great mystery <note>original: "Geheimniß"</note>. The description and the instruction for the preparation of these lights have moved me to include them here, even though I have not yet had the opportunity to investigate the truth of the matter through experiments of my own.

### The first manuscript contains the following:

Take 8 ounces <note>original: "Loth"; a historical German unit of weight, roughly 14.6 grams or half an ounce.</note> of sulfur, just as much calcined alum <term>calcined alum: alum that has been heated until it loses its water of crystallization, becoming a white, porous powder</term>, grind these two substances together, put the mixture into an earthenware sublimation vessel <term>sublimatorium: a specialized alchemical vessel used to heat a solid into a vapor so it can collect as a purified solid elsewhere</term>, seal it with lute <term>verlutire: to seal a vessel's joints with a paste (lute) to make it airtight before heating</term>, place it in a charcoal fire, and let the sulfur rise through the alum—

<summary>This page concludes the legend of Johannes Trithemius's perpetual lamp, which supposedly burned for twenty years in the Emperor's absence, and begins the first technical alchemical recipe for creating such a light using sulfur and alum.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, Emperor Maximilian I, everlasting light, sanctuary lamp, sulfur, alum, sublimation, alchemical apparatus</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for creating the "everlasting light" mentioned on the previous page, specifically concluding the chemical preparation of the sulfur and describing the assembly of the lamp using a fireproof wick.</meta>

...sublimate, and in 8 hours it will be prepared. From this, take at least 5 ounces <note>original: "Loth"; a historical German unit of weight, roughly 14.6 grams or half an ounce.</note>, and 2 ounces of crystalline borax; grind these two together, pour this into a flat glass vessel that lies level, pour a strong Spirit of Wine <note>original: "Spiritum Vini"; high-proof distilled alcohol</note> over it, and distill it off very gently in an ash bath <term>ash bath: a method of indirect heating where a vessel is placed in hot ashes to ensure a steady, low temperature</term> until it reaches an oily consistency; pour the spirit back onto it and distill it off once more. Take a little of the sulfur and place it on a glowing copper sheet; if it flows like wax, it is properly prepared; if not, you must distill more Spirit of Wine from it until it passes the test. Then take asbestos <term>Federweiß: literally "feather-white," a term usually referring to fibrous asbestos or talc, valued in alchemy for its fire-resistant properties</term> and make a wick or a prong from it, about as long as a little finger and half as thick; wrap this with white silk and place it whole into a small Venetian glass. To this, add the sulfur prepared above; set the glass in warm sand until the sulfur has melted and has settled above and below around the prong or wick, so that only a little bit of it is visible at the top. Light the wick with a common candle, and it will immediately begin to burn; the sulfur remains fluid in the glass. This can be placed wherever one wishes, and it will never go out.

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous section which described a method for creating an "everlasting light" using sulfur and asbestos. This entry, labeled as "the other manuscript," provides an alternative alchemical procedure using antimony to achieve a similar result.</meta>

( 130 )

### 105.

## The other manuscript teaches another way to prepare an inextinguishable light.

Take one pound of glass of antimony <term>glass of antimony: a transparent, amber-colored vitreous substance produced by roasting antimony ore, frequently used in alchemical preparations</term>, grind it very fine, and place it in a glass vessel. Pour vinegar over it—prepared as described further below—and set it in warm ashes; seal the top and let it stand for 6 hours, so that the vinegar draws out a yellow color. Pour this liquid off, add fresh vinegar, and repeat the process until no more yellow color appears. Then, let it evaporate <note>original: "abrauchen"; to smoke off or evaporate until only a residue remains</note> until it reaches an oily consistency. Purify this oily substance by rectifying it <term>rectify: to refine or purify a liquid through the process of distillation</term> in a water bath <term>water bath: also known as a Bain-Marie (original: "Balneo Mariæ"), a container of water used to heat another vessel gently and evenly</term>. Remove the body of antimony <note>original: "Corpus Antimonii"; the solid material remaining after the liquid has been drawn off</note>, dry it, and grind it fine. Place it in a glass, and pour the rectified oil over it. Distill it off and pour it back on, repeating this process seven times until the solid matter has "swallowed" the oil. Take this solid body, place it in a clean glass, and pour Spirit of Wine <note>original: "Spiritum Vini"; high-proof distilled alcohol</note> over it to extract the essence. Repeat this with fresh spirit until everything has been extracted. Place this into a Venetian glass <note>a high-quality, clear glass vessel prized by alchemists for its purity</note>, pour it through a five-fold layer of paper, and distill off the Spirit of Wine; thus, the incombustible oil remains at the bottom <note>original: "in fundo"</note>.

<summary>This page describes an alternative alchemical recipe for an "everlasting light" by extracting and purifying an incombustible oil from antimony using vinegar and Spirit of Wine. This refined oil was intended to burn indefinitely without being consumed.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, glass of antimony, inextinguishable light, alchemy, distillation, water bath, Spirit of Wine, Venetian glass, incombustible oil</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
