( 131 )

Prepare the vinegar in the following way: Take one pound of well-dried salt <note>original: "gedorrtes Salz"; salt that has been parched or heated to remove all moisture, making it more reactive for the distillation process</note>, pour a strong wine-vinegar over it, and draw it off <note>original: "ziehe... ab"; a term for distilling or evaporating a liquid so that it separates from the solid matter</note> until it reaches an oily consistency. Pour more vinegar over it, draw it off again, and repeat this process four times; in this way, you have prepared the vinegar that is necessary for this work.

Place it in a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "Sand Capelle"; a vessel filled with sand that sits between the fire and the glass to distribute heat evenly and prevent the glass from shattering</note> or, if one is not available, in an <term>earthenware bowl</term> which must be filled with some sand. Place it over a gentle coal fire and stir the thick <term>mixture</term> <note>original: "Mixtum"; Latin for a compound or blended substance</note> until it begins to boil. for other monks, his fellow brothers <note>original: *Conventualibus*; members of a religious order living in a convent or monastery</note>,

the likes of which can only be found in the 
writings <note>original: *Scriptis*</note> of true philosophers and is 
particularly noteworthy here.

For my part, I consider it to be such a 
writing, of which few are to be seen, fewer 
written, and the fewest of all can be 
understood or noticed. As it [happened] to me from a—

### The Priest
## *Antonius de Abbatia’s*
# Report on the Transformation of Metals.

Although I have mentioned much to you in my letters regarding the blessed Art <term>Art: Alchemy was frequently referred to simply as "The Art" or the "Hermetic Art"</term>, without which—as *Raymundus* <note>Raymundus: Likely referring to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and mystic to whom many influential alchemical works were attributed</note> says—the core of Philosophy cannot be perfectly attained; nevertheless, because I told you that if **GOD** grants me grace to discover something, especially regarding the Red <note>original: *aufs Rothe*; referring to the "Red Work" or *rubedo*, the final stage of the alchemical process which results in the philosopher's stone capable of transmuting metals into gold</note>, just as I have discovered it for the White <note>original: *aufs Weiße*; the "White Work" or *albedo*, the stage of the process that produces the tincture for transmuting metals into silver</note>, I wished to reveal this to you. Therefore, I have desired to attach this small portion to my epistle <note>epistle: a formal letter</note>, so that you may perceive the complete truth. I will, therefore, now fulfill with a complete account what I previously promised you, and I will not be begrudging or ungrateful, just as many have been before me; indeed, hardly anyone can be found who has not described the Art in a veiled <note>original: *verdeckter Weise*; referring to the alchemical tradition of using "Decknamen" or "cover-names" and metaphors to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note> manner.

By **GOD**! I will not be among their number, but rather I have compassion for you, and

<summary>Antonius de Abbatia begins his treatise by promising to provide a clear and honest explanation of alchemical transmutation. He specifically intends to reveal the secrets of both the "White" and "Red" stages of the work, criticizing earlier authors for being intentionally obscure.</summary>
<keywords>Antonius de Abbatia, transformation of metals, alchemy, Raymond Lull, Red Work, White Work, transmutation, secrecy</keywords>

...having been shared with a good friend who is well-versed in the reading of these books of the Art <term>Art: Alchemy was frequently referred to simply as "The Art" or the "Hermetic Art"</term>, I have not kept it for myself alone. Rather, I have translated it from Latin into German, wishing to share it with others who are diligent in the Art but unlearned in Latin, and to make it known through public printing. I do not doubt that, through diligent reflection, they will finally realize and hold fast to the purpose of the Monk <note>Monk: Referring to the author, the priest Antonius de Abbatia</note> and what the goal of the Art is capable of achieving.

I live in the complete confidence that the favorable reader and lover of the Art—following a prior invocation of **GOD**, and through persistent, diligent meditation <term>Meditation: In alchemy, this refers to deep internal reflection and spiritual study of the processes</term> and the subsequent experimental trials <term>experimental trials: original: *künstlichen Versuchung*; in this context, the word "Versuchung" refers to a practical experiment or laboratory trial (*Versuch*) rather than its modern meaning of "temptation"</term>—will infallibly obtain the goal and purpose of his desires. This is what has been left to us with good intentions by this Adept <note>Adept: original: *Adepto*; a person who has successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy</note> and philosopher <note>original: *Philosopho*</note>, who was highly enlightened and gifted by **GOD**. To this end, I wish the reader much fortune and salvation from the Most High.

### Of the Priest
## Antonius de Abbatia’s
# Report on the Transformation of Metals.

Although I have reported much to you in my letter concerning the blessed Art <term>Art: A common shorthand for Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art"</term>, without which—as Raymond <note>Raymond: Referring to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and logician to whom many alchemical works were later attributed</note> says—the core of Philosophy <note>original: *Philosophiae*; referring here to "Natural Philosophy," the study of the physical world which included alchemical science</note> cannot be perfectly attained; nevertheless, because I told you that if **GOD** grants me grace that I might discover something—primarily regarding the Red <term>the Red: Known as the *Rubedo*, this is the final stage of the Great Work, which results in the "Red Stone" capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term>, as I have discovered it for the White <term>the White: Known as the *Albedo*, the stage preceding the final goal, which produces a "White Stone" capable of transmuting metals into silver</term>—I wished to reveal this to you. Therefore, I have wanted to attach these few words to my epistle <note>epistle: a formal letter</note> so that you may perceive the complete truth. I will now fulfill that promise with a complete narrative of what I previously promised you, and I do not wish to be begrudging or ungrateful, as many before me have been—indeed, hardly anyone is found who has not described the Art in a veiled manner <note>veiled manner: Alchemists traditionally used "Decknamen" (cover-names) and cryptic allegories to prevent their secrets from being understood by the unworthy or "uninitiated"</note>.

By **GOD**! I do not wish to be among their number, but rather have compassion for you, and

<summary>This page serves as the opening of the priest Antonius de Abbatia’s alchemical treatise, where he promises to reveal the secrets of the "Red" and "White" stones plainly, rather than in the cryptic language used by other philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Antonius de Abbatia, Raymond Lull, alchemy, transmutation, Red Stone, White Stone, Great Work, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical introduction of Antonius de Abbatia's treatise. The author moves from his personal promise to a direct address to the reader, emphasizing the need for a foundational understanding of natural philosophy before attempting the specific transformations of the "White" and the "Red."</meta>

<header>✥ ✥ ( 6 ) ✥ ✥</header>

Therefore, I wish to explain all secrets to you as much as is possible, and I will show you how the White <term>the White: The *Albedo* stage, which results in the ability to create silver</term> proceeds, and how to make the Yellow <term>the Yellow: *Citrininitas*, a transitional stage of "yellowing" that occurs before the final goal</term> turn red <term>red: The *Rubedo*, the final stage of the Great Work which produces the Philosopher's Stone</term>, which is the secret work of the Sages <note>Sages: The master alchemists or "Wise Men" who preceded the author</note>.

   Listen closely, therefore, so that nothing I say passes you by; for I intend to teach you something now that has never been brought forth from a human mouth so brightly and clearly.

   Therefore, I exhort you above all things to practice diligently in the fundamental sciences <note>original: *gründlichen Wiſſenſchaften*; in this context, the study of the laws of nature and the properties of matter</note>, so that you may properly recognize the origin of natural things, as well as their causes and elements <note>original: *Elementa*; refers to the four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>. Also, [you must learn] when and how nature proceeds in an orderly and uniform manner from the essence of natural things through movement and natural causes.

   In this way, you will then easily be able to draw a true understanding from the words of the philosophers, who have composed their books and speeches with signs <note>signs: Symbolic characters and cryptic language used to hide alchemical secrets from the "unworthy"</note>, so that these might serve as a reminder to themselves, or to encourage their descendants to investigate this high and excellent science. They described it in parables <note>original: *Gleichniſſen*; allegories or metaphors used to mask chemical processes as stories</note> so that it would not be lost entirely.

   Thus it has come to pass that their books cannot be understood except by those who have actually performed the work <note>original: *ſie gemachet haben*; literally "have made them," implying those who have successfully completed the alchemical experiments themselves</note>.

   For this reason, some of the philosophers have also said that working according to the plain letter <note>original: *klaren Buchſtaben*; the literal, surface-level meaning of the text without understanding the hidden allegories</note>...

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>The author promises to reveal the secrets of the White and Red stages of alchemy and emphasizes that the "fundamental sciences" and natural laws must be understood to decode the symbolic language of previous philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Albedo, Rubedo, Citrininitas, natural philosophy, four elements, alchemical symbols, allegories, transmission of knowledge</keywords>

...[so that it] would be a waste of resources and the ruin of the work.

Therefore, I exhort you that you should direct all your thoughts—those you have gathered from the books of the philosophers or from your own mind through your imagination—toward the natural principles. Just as goldsmiths do, who test metals on a black touchstone <term>touchstone: A hard, dark stone used to test the purity of gold and silver by the color of the streak left when rubbed against it</term> by rubbing or striking: so you too should test your opinions and imaginings against natural principles and confirm them with the foundations of philosophy <note>original: *Philosophiae*; here referring to natural philosophy or the scientific study of the physical world</note>.

When you thus come to understand the Art <note>the Art: Alchemy, often referred to as "The Royal Art" or "The Great Art"</note>, especially when the sayings of the wise agree with your own imagination, then it will be correct.

And this is exactly what the Master of the Art from Florence <note>Master of Florence: Likely a reference to a Florentine authority in the Hermetic tradition, possibly Marsilio Ficino or a similar figure</note> wished for you to know when he said that the practitioner should compare the sayings of the philosophers against one another and unite them.

So too does the highly experienced and famous Raymundus <note>Raymundus: Referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic whose works were highly influential in later alchemy</note> teach in his Art, that one must lead that which is sought back around in a circular manner <note>original: *Cirkels-weise*; likely refers to the alchemical process of "circulation," a repetitive distillation meant to refine a substance</note> according to the order of universal principles.

In this, the truth of the whole essence is recognized no differently than the image of the one looking into a mirror is seen.

In sum, I say to you that unless you first know all the opinions of the philosophers

of whom there are very few, they nevertheless mix
the same with other foreign things so much
that they ruin it and bring it to nothing.

Therefore, one must leave them stuck in their error, for it is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a needle than for such people to achieve anything good. For this reason, *Raymundus* <note>Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull, c. 1232–1315), a major philosopher to whom many foundational alchemical works were attributed</note> said in his *Testament* that such sophists <term>sophists: in an alchemical context, "sophists" or "sophisticators" were charlatans who used deceptive tricks or false logic rather than following the true laws of nature</term> can never attain the knowledge <note>original: *Wissenschaft*; in the 17th century, this referred to a systematic body of certain knowledge or "scientia," rather than modern lab science</note>.

Likewise, *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–813), known as the "Father of Chemistry." His Latinized works were the standard textbooks for European alchemists</note>, a layman who was a master of masters in the Art, set down these golden words regarding this opinion in his *Summa* <note>original: *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii*, or "The Height of the Perfection of Mastery," the most famous alchemical work of the Middle Ages</note>: The seeker of this Art should not strive to invent a sophistic goal for the work, but should always look toward a correct continuation of the work; thus he will strike upon the true Art, and not be a sophist. For our Art is kept within the power of **GOD**, and He grants it to whom He wills, and also withdraws it—He who is highly praised and full of righteousness and goodness.

For perhaps **GOD** the **LORD**, as punishment for your sophistic work, might deny you the Art and plunge you into devious error, and from error into misfortune and perpetual misery. For he is very wretched and unfortunate whom **GOD**, after the completion of his work and labor, does not permit to see the truth, and who must end and conclude his lifetime in sadness. He is placed in constant sorrow, and surrounded by all misfortune and misery, [and] will [be deprived] of all comfort, joy...

<meta>This page continues the warning from the previous page, describing the spiritual and emotional ruin of those who pursue alchemy with false intentions. <page-num>10</page-num>

...and deprived of the joy of this world, and must end his life in sorrow.

Also the Master of the Art from Florence <note>likely referring to a Renaissance figure such as Marsilio Ficino or a similar authority associated with the Florentine Hermetic tradition</note>, in the preface of his book, sets down this saying: There are many obstacles to this famous science <note>original: *Wissenschaft*; in this period, it referred to a systematic body of certain knowledge rather than modern laboratory science</note> which hinder the artist, especially when he falls into despair and turns to performing a sophistic work <term>sophistic work: deceptive or fraudulent chemical operations that mimic true results but lack the substance of nature</term>, against which one must completely guard oneself. The very same author speaks in the 11th chapter at the end of the second part, stating that in the right exploration of this Art, all sophistic work is rejected, and that it has no enemies but the ignorant <note>original: *Ignoranten*; this refers to the common alchemical adage *Ars non habet inimicum nisi ignorantem* (Art has no enemy but the ignorant)</note>.

Furthermore, the late Thomas Aquinas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274); while a renowned theologian, many alchemical treatises were attributed to him in the late medieval and early modern periods to lend them authority</note>, concerning the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: *Turbam Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient sages</note>, began thus from the mouth of the all-wise Solomon: "My mouth shall speak the truth, and my lips shall mock the godless" <note>A paraphrase of Proverbs 8:7</note>; and he says that in the aforementioned words, he wishes to demonstrate a twofold effect of wisdom: namely, the truth of the Alchemical Art, which is a contemplation of the principles of nature—which is indicated when he says: "My mouth shall compose of the truth"—and a triumph over the errors that are against the truth, which he touches upon when he says: "And my lips shall curse the godless," that is, the falsifier. And he adds the reason for this, since it is the same thing, regarding two opposing matters, to pursue the one and to refute the other: just as medicine does, which [restores] health...

reintroduces [health], and drives out the sickness; therefore also Aristotle has said in the *Elenchis* <note>original: *Elenchis*; referring to *Sophistici Elenchi* (Sophistical Refutations), where Aristotle discusses logical fallacies</note>, that two things are expected of a wise man: first, that he should present no falsehood; and second, that if a falsehood is presented, he should expose it.

But let us return to our purpose. Thus you should now know that the true Materia <term>Materia: the raw substance or "First Matter" used in the alchemical process</term> is obtained from the thing which Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval alchemy</note>, as I have told you, has called in an Epistle "the Black blacker than Black" <note>original: *das Schwarze schwärzer als Schwarz*; a reference to the *Nigredo* or blackening stage, the initial phase of the alchemical process</note>, and it is nothing other than our slimy water which comes from a strong wine, and which, as Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says, is taken from the hidden caves of the minerals.

But after the conjunction <gloss>composition</gloss> it is called a permanent water, the persistent Black; and thereafter it becomes a nourisher, root, trunk, foundation, *Hyle* <term>Hyle: a term from Greek philosophy meaning "matter" or "raw substance," used here to denote the primordial state of the work</term>, a fragrant branch, our composition <term>compositum: the resulting mixture or substance of the Work</term> of clear water, everlasting water, growing water of *Balaci* <note>likely referring to the *Balas* ruby or a specific alchemical solvent</note>, first Materia, the seed of metals, universal Chaos, the hermaphroditic gender <note>representing the union of masculine and feminine principles, or Sulfur and Mercury</note>, the conceiving virgin, our *Chelidonia* <gloss>greater celandine</gloss> and *Lunaria major* <gloss>honesty or moonwort</gloss>, and is called by many other names by the philosophers in their books. <meta>This page continues from page 11, where the author began discussing the "First Matter" (Materia) and its various aliases. The text previously mentioned "slimy water" and the *Nigredo* (blackness) stage, setting the stage for the current discussion on the confusing variety of names used by ancient masters to describe a single substance.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

...that nothing leads practitioners <note>original: *Artisten*; referring to the alchemists or practitioners of the "Great Art"</note> into more error than the variety of names, though no one experienced in the Art has ever been troubled by them. Therefore, you should also not let yourself be led astray, but always keep in mind that it is one single thing, even if it is called by different and almost innumerable names.

For this reason, it is also commanded in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between pre-Socratic philosophers</note> that one should abandon the many obscure names.

Hence Fristes <note>A speaker appearing in the *Turba Philosophorum*</note> says in the *Turba*:
> I swear to you by God, that I have searched through books for a long time so that I might come to the knowledge of this thing, and I have prayed to God that He would teach me what it is. But when He heard my prayer, He showed me a water, which I know to be true vinegar <note>original: *Essig*; in alchemy, "philosophical vinegar" refers to a powerful acidic solvent or the "mercurial water" that dissolves metals</note>; and the more I read the books, the more clearly and brightly the truth of this blessed water appeared.

And the Master of the Art from Florence <note>Magister artis de Florentia; likely a reference to a Renaissance-era alchemical authority</note> says in Chapter 2:
> "But it is a secret of this Art, that one should know and recognize the heavenly, glorious water of which we spoke above, which transforms our body into a spirit."

Also Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy</note>, in the chapter on the properties of Mars <note>original: *Martis*; the alchemical symbol for iron</note>, extols the same under the name of Mercury <note>original: *Mercurii*; the alchemical symbol for quicksilver, often representing the fluid, transformative principle</note>, and says: 
> "Praised be the Most High, Most Glorious and Blessed, who created this thing, and gave it a substance, and gave the substance such proper—"

<page-num>13</page-num>

...properties <note>original: *genschafften*; likely the conclusion of *Eigenschaften* from the previous page</note>, which one cannot have or obtain from other things in nature, just as this perfection can be found within it through a masterpiece <note>original: *Kunststück*; here referring to the "Great Work" of alchemy</note>, which we have found to possess the most immediate power for this purpose. For it is this which overcomes the fire, and is not overwhelmed by it, but rests in it friendly and with joy.

Why should one bring in so many other testimonies! Do not all the testimonies of the philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the long lineage of alchemical authorities, from antiquity through the Middle Ages</note> cry out that a beginning of the Art is made from this same water, and is also finished with the same?

We must not let ourselves be talked out of this, nor allow it to be interpreted differently than it is in itself according to the will of another who would wish to instruct us; for as you see, it is a work of Nature, which will by no means allow itself to be regulated according to the whims of our understanding. On the contrary, our understanding must instead apply itself to conforming to it, since truth is nothing other than an equality of the thing with the understanding <note>original: *Gleichheit des Dinges mit dem Verstande*; a translation of the Scholastic Latin definition of truth: *Adaequatio rei et intellectus*</note>, according to the opinion of Aristotle.

To this affirmation of ours, the honorable Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and polymath whose works on nature were foundational to alchemical theory</note> also gives his approval in the third book of *On Minerals* <note>original: *von den Bergarten*; referring to the treatise *De mineralibus*</note>, Chapter 2, which he concludes thus: that the first matter <note>original: *Materia*</note> of metals is nothing other than something moist, greasy, and subtle, which is strongly mixed and incorporated with an earthly subtlety, so that there is an equal amount of both.

Therefore I say to you, my brothers and sons, believe me, that you do not need to rack your brains over other things, except only the blessed water. Because this work, as a philosopher <note>original: *Philosophus*; referring to an authority in the alchemical tradition</note> says, is nothing else but the boiling of our permanent water, until you see that it remains fixed and stable, and no longer rises <note>likely referring to the process of stabilization where the substance no longer evaporates or "flees" the heat</note>.

And when you know how to work <note>original: *laboriren*; to perform the chemical operations of the laboratory</note> correctly, then you will see our vegetable salt <note>the vital essence extracted from plants</note>, our gold made spiritual, our silver, our lead, our tin, our copper, our iron, our arsenic, our sal ammoniac <note>original: *Salarmeniacum*; ammonium chloride, used as a flux</note>, our vitriol <note>a sulfate of iron or copper</note>, our magnesia <note>in alchemy, not modern magnesium, but often a reference to a transformative mineral or "the black lead"</note>, our alum, our kybrit <note>original: *Kybrit*; an Arabic-derived term for sulfur</note>, our sulfur, our milk, vinegar, and child's urine, the white honey, the fountain:

The dew of heavenly grace, the sourest vinegar, our wine, our spicy solvent <note>original: *Menstruum*; a substance that dissolves a solid body</note>, the blessed drink of the gods, and many such things, all of which take their origin and first essence from this same water or compound <note>original: *composito*</note>. After it is purified and prepared through our art, it thus transforms itself into different shapes, and is turned into various medicines <note>original: *Artzney*</note>, which yet are always, according to their main ground or origin, one and the same.

And indeed, such a thing is not absurd, for it is certainly explained <note>original: *erkläh*; a fragment of *erkläret* (explained), acting as a catchword for the next page</note> sufficiently in the books of the philosophers...

explains <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely continuing from a name like Albertus Magnus on the previous page</note> that the difference of the *matter* <note>original: *Materii*</note> does not indicate a difference in the thing itself.

Do not be astonished, you dearly beloved brothers, that I have called this water a *compound* <note>original: *Compositum*; a substance made of multiple parts</note> or something put together, for I aim to reveal everything to you thoroughly, and on my conscience, I do not desire to keep anything hidden for myself, but rather give everything forth to you, according to the command of the Lord, where he speaks:

> What you have received for free, give back for free. <note>A reference to Matthew 10:8: "Freely ye have received, freely give."</note>

One part, which rises into the height <note>referring to the volatile part that evaporates or distills</note>, is called "ours"; the other part, which remains below, is called the *fixed* <note>original: *Fixe*; stable and resistant to heat</note> part. These two parts are otherwise called earth and *ferment* <note>original: *Ferment*; a substance used to "leaven" or transform the base material</note>.

Therefore, some *philosophers* <note>original: *Philosophi*; here meaning alchemists</note>, who have looked upon these two parts, have said that the same water is found upon two mountains.

And you should know that the two parts are found separately in their *minerals* <note>original: *Mineris*; the raw ores or source materials</note>; that is, not properly and well mixed. This is because a single *form* <note>original: *forma*; in the Aristotelian sense, the essential nature or "blueprint" of a substance</note> alone, as is stated here, cannot apply uniformly to each part of the *compound* <note>original: *Compositi*</note>. Therefore, through manual labor <note>physical laboratory work</note>, although for different purposes, we ensure that each of the aforementioned parts flows out separately, after which puri= <note>catchword: -fy; the text likely continues with "purifying"</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the alchemical "compound" (Compositum). The previous page ended by discussing the two parts of the "blessed water"—the volatile and the fixed—which are here further identified with the principles of Sulfur and Mercury (Quick-silver).</meta>

...[purif]y each part individually through philosophical work, and following that, we join them together until something fixed and fluid remains, and a new *compound* <note>original: *Compositum*</note> is generated. From this, gain understanding, if you are indeed subtle researchers of the Art <note>The "Art" is a traditional term for Alchemy</note>.

Therefore, Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a renowned 8th-century figure considered the father of early chemistry</note> placed the perfection of the *Mastery* <term>Magisterium</term> <note>original: *Magisterii*; refers to the completed alchemical process or "The Great Work"</note> neither in sulfur alone, nor in *quick-silver* <term>argento vivo</term> <note>Latin for "living silver"; Mercury</note> alone, but says that each of them can enter into the preparation of the *Stone* <term>Lapis</term> <note>original: *Lapidis*; short for the Philosopher's Stone</note>. The reason is because our *compound* <term>Compositum</term> is not made until after the joining of these two parts, from which, as from a father and mother, our *Philosopher's Stone* <term>Lapis philos.</term> originates.

For the volatile part, which Geber calls *quick-silver* <term>arg. vivum</term> because of its similarity, cannot be so hardened that it becomes the Stone, because it lacks a fatty substance, which is the cause of hardness. Likewise, the fixed part, which Geber calls *Sulfur* <term>Sulphur</term>, can be made into the Stone by no art, and this is because it has no moisture within it, by which the parts might hang so dissolvably together, as if they could be poured like a glue, without the destruction (loss) of the other.

From what I have said, it is evident that both—namely the moist and the dry—are ordered toward the metallic nature, which is otherwise called a *dry water*, and is the entire path to this masterpiece, and its perfect—

He argues that neither element can succeed alone, as one lacks hardness and the other lacks the binding moisture necessary for the "dry water."</summary>

...common end. And this is known to all sages in this Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy</note>, namely that the matter which we take in hand is not made perfect except solely by means of digestion <term>digestion</term> <note>In alchemy, digestion refers to the slow, gentle heating of a substance in a sealed vessel, mimicking the biological process</note>. Now, nothing can be digested unless it is digestible. That which is digestible primarily contains two parts within it: a moist and a dry, as Aristotle confirms in his established description in the 4th book of *Meteorology* <note>Aristotle’s *Meteorologica*, Book IV, was the foundational text for medieval theories on how heat acts upon matter</note>. For he says that digestion is a passage to perfection brought about by its own and natural heat, acting through the opposing passive quality. Whereby he understands by the "opposing passive quality"—as it is commonly explained in that work—the moist which is mixed with the dry. For that which is actually and primarily digested in the moist becomes dry through the mixture; by means of this, according to its varying mixture, it is digested by the natural heat.

However, the moist undergoes no digestion without the dry; therefore it is stated there that water cannot be made thick through moisture alone.

Oh, how beautifully a Philosopher has spoken, saying that these two parts—namely, the Moist and the Dry—are called the honored stones, which originally descend from one father, and are two things according to their names, and yet in form they are only one, through which the secret can be naturally brought about, just as

Man and woman, though they are two by name, are yet only one in form for the purpose of begetting a son. If the common man knew this, he would not hold them in low esteem.

Thus you now have, my most beloved brothers, everything that you seek; and if you rightly and well understand what I have said, the error of those will become manifest to you who wish to bring about the unique compound <note>original: *Compositum*</note> from the two aforementioned thin and clear [substances] at the beginning of the work through philosophical labor.

The Philosopher <note>A common title for Aristotle in alchemical and scholastic texts</note> proves in his books of *Philosophical Observation*, book 1 <note>original: *lib. 1.*, likely referring to Aristotle's *Physics*</note>, that there are three principles of natural things: namely, matter <note>original: *Materia*</note>, form <note>original: *Forma*</note>, and deprivation <note>original: *Privatio*; in Aristotelian philosophy, this refers to the absence of a form that a substance is capable of possessing</note>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the three principles of nature—matter, form, and privation—drawing on Aristotelian philosophy to explain how the alchemical transformation occurs. It specifically addresses why the old form of a substance must be destroyed before the "excellent form" of the Philosopher's Stone can be achieved.</meta>

has an active essence of its own, except solely through **form** <term>form</term> <note>original: *Formam*; in Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" is the essential nature or organizing principle of a thing</note>, and is a **subject** <term>subject</term> <note>original: *subjectum*; the underlying matter that "stands under" or receives different qualities</note> of all forms one after another; that is, it always possesses one form in actuality, or presently, and the others in potential, although a distinction also arises here, as some of the aforementioned forms are closer in potential, while others are more distant. Therefore, if one wishes to proceed from one form to another, **privation** <term>privation</term> <note>original: *Privatio*; the state of being "deprived" of a form; the necessary void or transitional state between the loss of one form and the gain of another</note> must necessarily intervene. Because this privation follows the previous expelled form and opens the way for the introduction of a new form, it is primarily counted by **the Philosopher** <note>original: *Philosopho*; referring to Aristotle, whom alchemists regarded as the ultimate authority on natural physics</note> among the natural principles.

I have found it necessary to repeat this because there are some fools who presume to introduce this excellent form of the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>original: *Lapidis Phil.*, an abbreviation of *Lapidis Philosophorum*</note> into our **compound** <term>compound</term> <note>original: *Compositum*; the mixture of ingredients in the alchemical vessel</note>, and yet they do not know how to strip it of its previous form.

But I tell you, where no **destruction** <note>original: *Zerstörung*</note> occurs, no new **generation** <note>original: *Zeugung*; refers here to the "birthing" or creation of a new substance</note> can happen. A very clear example can be found in the General Judgment, which shall be held at the end of time by the command of the Creator; in this, everything shall be destroyed by fire down to the first **matter** <term>matter</term> <note>original: *matery*; the "materia prima" or the most basic, formless building block of the universe</note>. And after such a burning, the bodies of men shall rise incorruptible: and the **mastery of nature** <term>mastery of nature</term> <note>original: *magisterium naturæ*; the alchemical process or the "Great Work"</note> follows just such a process.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "compound" (Compositum) and the role of the alchemist in relation to nature. It follows the previous page's explanation that a substance must lose its form (privation) before it can receive the excellent form of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

My dear brothers, take note everyone and read these words diligently, for a great wisdom is hidden within them.

Yet another error shall be revealed to you concerning this **compound** <term>compound</term> <note>original: *Composito*; the mixture of substances in the alchemical vessel</note> of ours, from which other **compounds** <note>original: *Composita*</note> are brought forth from our composite material. For some have presumed to bring the same about anew, which one cannot possibly achieve, because it is a **work of nature** <term>work of nature</term> <note>original: *Werk der Natur*</note>, and is scarcely produced by nature alone without the assistance of **Art** <term>Art</term> <note>original: *Kunst*; the skill and laboratory practice of the alchemist</note> in a thousand years. Afterward, Art takes this same thing and works within it so that it shortens the work of nature, and fulfills and brings to an end in a single day—that is, in a short time—what nature alone could scarcely do in a thousand years.

In the same manner, you may know from the preceding the reason why I made **white gold** <term>white gold</term> <note>original: *weisses Gold*; likely an intermediate state or a specific silver-gold alloy that was not yet the perfected Stone</note>, as I wrote to you in my letter. The reason was this: because I took only a **volatile** [substance] <term>volatile</term> <note>original: *Flüchtigen*; a substance that easily evaporates or escapes the fire</note> with its **greenness** <term>greenness</term> <note>original: *Grünigkeit*; a reference to the "Green Lion" or the raw, acidic energy of mineral life</note>, which came to my hand in the work through God's providence; and I combined it with one of the **great lights** <term>great lights</term> <note>original: *grossen Liechtern*; alchemical code for the Sun (gold) or the Moon (silver)</note>, and **labored** <note>original: *laboriret*</note> therein in a sophisticated manner <note>original: *sophistischer Art*; here suggesting a technical or clever method that, as the author later admits, was not the true "philosophical" path</note>. Therefore, after some time, when I had read **Archelaus's Epistle** <note>original: *Archelai Epistel*; a reference to a famous alchemical text attributed to the Greek philosopher Archelaus</note>, I realized that the work which I have just described to you was not according to the true meaning of the **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophen*; the traditional term for the master alchemists who held the secret of the Stone</note>.

Therefore, I later corrected my error, studied day and night, and constantly poured out my prayer before God the Lord, asking Him to lead me onto the right path, which finally happened through His goodness and grace; for this reason, His mercy shall be forever lauded and praised by me.

> “O dear sons, you will find it very useful, both at the beginning and in the progress of the work, to place the fresh water upon the common great lights—that is, **Sun** <note>original: *Solis*; gold</note> and **Moon** <note>original: *Lunæ*; silver</note>—inasmuch as you will find three benefits therein. And when it is drawn off from the bodies by means of a **helm** <term>helm</term> <note>original: *Helm*; the top, "helmet-like" part of a still or alembic used to collect vapor during distillation</note>, the spirits remain in the water, which then dissolves more readily and more quickly. Secondly, it also attains a greater **subtlety** <term>subtlety</term> <note>original: *Subtilität*; a state of being highly refined, purified, or "thinned" from its gross material form</note> and a retention of the spirits, which do not perish once they have joined with the body. Thirdly, **Sun and Moon** will also increase in weight; this multiplication can be sufficient for a **philosopher** <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: *Philosophus*; a practitioner of alchemy or the "Hermetic Philosophy"</note> to support himself during the work until the **Stone** <term>Stone</term> <note>original: *Lapis*; the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary catalyst for transmutation and healing</note> is finished.

Therefore a philosopher has said:
> You shall **impregnate** <note>original: *imprægniren*</note> the water with a body from which you may live until the **Stone** <note>original: *Lapis*</note> is completed.

may know how to guard yourself, you must read the books of the philosophers much and often, and primarily <term>Arnold of Villanova</term> <note>original: *Arnoldum de Villa nova*; a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> in his great <term>Rosary</term> <note>original: *Rosario*; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* or "Rose Garden of the Philosophers," one of the most influential alchemical texts of the Middle Ages</note>, and the <term>Master of the Art of Florence</term> <note>original: *Magistrum Artis de Florentia*; likely a reference to a specific Italian alchemical authority cited in late medieval collections</note>, especially in the 51st chapter, in which he very shrewdly examines all the work of the great <term>Stone</term> <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and explains it clearly to one who does not have a fantastical <note>meaning delusional or overly imaginative</note> understanding.

Therefore, I conclude and say that the entire work of the artist is that he may understand and know the new <term>compound</term> <note>original: *Compositum*; a chemically unified substance</note>, and how we compose the same by destroying that very lowly thing—namely, the first <term>compound</term> of nature—through manual labor, so that we may obtain the two parts, the **fixed** and **unfixed** <note>original: *Fixe und Unfixe*; refers to the solid, heat-stable matter and the volatile, vaporous spirits</note>, from whose mixture is born the **King**, who sits upon his throne crowned with his crown, in whom are the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*; alchemical gold</note> and the **Moon** <note>original: *Luna*; alchemical silver</note> and the other planets <note>the other five base metals: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> according to the desire and skill of the worker; and it is **triangular** in essence and **square** in quality or property. <note>This refers to the alchemical "squaring of the circle" or the geometric representation of the elements and the soul</note>

This is our highly exalted <term>Stone</term>, of which <term>Olybius</term> <note>a legendary figure in alchemical lore, often associated with the discovery of hidden chemical secrets</note> has stated in his *Compendium* that its origin is two-colored: namely, **white** and **green**.

## Praise of Wisdom from the Divine Holy Scripture.

Wisdom <note>original: "Sapient."; referring to the Wisdom of Solomon</note>, Chapter 6, verse 13. For wisdom is beautiful and imperishable, and is gladly seen by those who love her, and found by those who seek her. Indeed, she meets and makes herself known to those who desire her. Whoever would quickly have her need not take much trouble; he will find her waiting for him at his door. For to strive after her is the right prudence, and whoever is wakeful for her sake need not worry for long. For she goes about and seeks those who are worthy of her, and gladly appears to them along the way, and takes heed of them so that she might meet them.

# Preface
## To the reader who loves art and truth.

It is said that in these last times of the world <note>The author refers to the common 18th-century belief that the world was in its final age, often associated with a final revealing of hidden divine secrets.</note>, nothing is being written that has not been written before. If anyone may rightfully make such a claim regarding any art, I write the same with good reason concerning the incomparable art of making the **Tincture of the Physicians** <note>original: "Tinctur Physicorum"; also known as the Physical Tincture, a legendary alchemical medicine believed to possess universal curative powers.</note> or the **Philosopher's Stone**. For many hundreds of years—and indeed, from the first man **Adam** <note>Alchemists often claimed that their knowledge was "Prisca Sapientia" (ancient wisdom) granted by God to Adam in the Garden of Eden.</note> onward—there have always been certain men, thoroughly experienced in this highest art and highly enlightened by God. Although these men lived in lands and islands far distant from one another, spoke entirely different languages, and lived at different times, they have nevertheless always agreed in their descriptions—both regarding the material from which the **Stone** <note>original: "Lapis"</note> and **Tincture** <note>original: "Tinctura"</note> are made, as well as the necessary steps for their preparation. They agree so perfectly that it is as if a single person were the **author** <note>original: "Author"</note> of all their books; the only difference is that one used different words, and another a different **style** <note>original: "Styli"</note> or **method** <note>original: "Methodus"</note> to reveal his gifts. This fact is beyond doubt among true **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis"; in alchemical literature, "philosopher" almost always refers to an alchemist or a student of nature's hidden laws.</note>; therefore, he who correctly **under-** <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "stehet" at the bottom of the page indicates the word continues as "verstehet" (understands) on the following page.</note> a sincere **philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophum"</note>...

...stands, who, having described this art, may truthfully boast that he knows as much as is necessary of the contents of the books of all righteous philosophers; and this cannot be otherwise, because **Nature is one and simple** <note>original: "Natura una et simplex"; a fundamental alchemical principle stating that despite the complexity of the world, all matter originates from a single, unified source and follows a single set of laws.</note>, which still obediently and faithfully carries out the command received from its Creator at the **Creation** <note>original: "Schöpffung"</note> without any change or wavering, just as she has performed it from the beginning and at all times, according to which the philosophers model their work.

Now the curious reader might ask, or at least think to himself, what then moved the author of this little work to trouble his fellow men once again with this booklet, since so many magnificent old and new writings on this art are available in print—such as those by the praiseworthy <term>Sendivogius</term> <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and chemist known for his work on "central nitre."</note>, <term>Basilius Valentinus</term> <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, supposedly a Benedictine monk, whose influential works focused on the medicinal uses of antimony.</note>, <term>Jacob Böhme</term> <note>A German Christian mystic and theologian who used alchemical metaphors to describe the spiritual transformation of the soul.</note>, <term>Count Trevisan</term> <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who allegedly spent his life and fortune seeking the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, and <term>Theophrastus Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> (whom I name specifically for good reason) and countless others.

I provide the answer on behalf of the author to my especially great patrons, and in truth it is this: that it was done partly out of love toward sincerely minded seekers or doubting enthusiasts, to fully instruct and respectively strengthen them according to God's will; and partly out of a desire, with those already enlightened and [blessed] by God...

Concerning Nature, let it not be withheld how GOD the Almighty founded and planted his magnificent building, the Great World <note>The Macrocosm, or the universe as a whole, as opposed to the Microcosm (man).</note>, upon four main pillars, namely the four elements <note>original: "Elementa." And this earth is nothing else but a coarse water; the water a coarse air; and the air, a coarse fire; just as the fire may be called a subtle air, the air a subtle water, and the water a subtle earth. Thus, one element always acts upon the other—indeed, one generates within the other, so that one might transmutate <note>original: "transmutire." The alchemical concept of changing one substance into another through the rearrangement of its elemental qualities.</note> the other. For since GOD the Almighty [made] the fire hot and dry—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "three beginnings" (Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt) mentioned at the end of the previous page, explaining their origins in the divine creation of the elements and their practical necessity in alchemical work.</meta>

✥ ✥ ✥ 46 ✥ ✥ ✥

Furthermore, it is to be known that when God the Lord created the **elements**, He set the Fire to work against the Air, and this labor brought forth **Sulfur**; the Air worked within the Water, and this working brought forth **Mercury** <term>Mercurium; in alchemy, this is the "spirit" or fluid principle of matter</term>; the Water worked against the Earth, and from this came forth the **Salt**. These **three beginnings** <note>original: "drey Anfänge"; these are the Tria Prima of Paracelsian alchemy: Sulfur (soul), Mercury (spirit), and Salt (body)</note> are perfect and are to be found in all things. Within them is their **elixir** or **seed** <note>original: "Sperma"</note>, which is much more useful to a **Philosopher** <note>Meaning a student of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy</note> than the four elements <note>original: "elementa"</note>. For even if someone [possessed the elements themselves], he would be able to do nothing else with them than to produce the three aforementioned beginnings from them.

Therefore, I well-meaningly advise any right-minded lover of this Art of **Physical Nature** <note>original: "Naturæ Physicæ"</note> not to take the four elements, but rather to take the **beginnings** of each thing, and to take them in succession for whatever purpose he wishes to have or use them. For whoever does not do this, and wishes to complete his work with the very first [matter of creation], will certainly not succeed. Such a person reaches [too far] into the work of the Great God, which He has reserved for Himself: [the power] to create and sustain everything from the first **materials** <note>original: "M[aterien]"</note> or four elements.

For this reason, he acts more wisely who takes the closest path, taking in hand the most immediate means of creation, and considers how the Lord [created] apples, pears, herbs,

However, the reader must not understand me as speaking of common red gold; that gold is dead. The gold of the *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophen"; referring to alchemists who seek the underlying truths of nature</note>, however, is living. Just as no meat or any other food is useful to the human body without preparation—that is, while raw and uncooked—but must first be boiled and properly prepared, so the *philosophers* have also concluded that their gold and its seed must first be excellently prepared and boiled in its own water, if it is to accomplish something more and better than common gold. I write again: a *rebirth* <note>original: "Wiedergeburth"; a central concept in both alchemy and mysticism, referring to the purification and transformation of a substance</note> through water and spirit is necessary for this. Every creature returns again to that from which it came, namely to its mother; and ultimately it returns, according to its opportunity, to GOD, from whom it first went out through the Word, as into the *great mystery* <note>original: "mysterium magnum"; a term popularized by Paracelsus referring to the primordial "great chaos" or the undifferentiated source of all matter</note>, so that all things remain in unity, and are sustained and preserved by One, namely by GOD.

I must not write more clearly, otherwise I would present myself as a loathsome example or a *monstrosity* <note>original: "Mißgeburth"; literally a "misbirth" or deformity</note> before all true *philosophers*, and make myself utterly hated; and then—what is most to be feared—I might bring God’s wrath and punishment upon my own neck.

As he confidentially told me, he received it from a **Belgian** <note>original: "Belgico"</note> on his deathbed in **Amsterdam**; the first name given for this Belgian was **Anton**, and the first three letters of his surname, along with the little word **DE NOZ**, were [unclear];

However, let the one who is not experienced in this art and not yet worthy of it be warned not to ask after my person and name: for against such a person I am constantly determined to keep **Pythagorean silence** <note>original: "Pytagoricum silentium"; an ancient vow of absolute secrecy practiced by the followers of Pythagoras</note>, and to allow him no instruction in this art, no matter how it may go for me. Yet, although more than many other **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophi"; referring to alchemists as "natural philosophers"</note> have been cited above for the instruction of those artists still seeking, I will nevertheless add two more **Enigmas** <note>original: "Ænigmata"; riddles or allegorical puzzles used to hide alchemical secrets from the uninitiated</note> below as a supplement, and first present their best concept now, so that the aforementioned **Philosophical Nuptials** <term>Philosophisch Beylager: the "Chemical Wedding" or the symbolic union of substances, such as sulfur and mercury, to create the Philosopher's Stone</term> might not be misunderstood in our art,

<summary>The author recounts how he came into possession of Paracelsian secrets through a deceased friend who received them from a mysterious Belgian in Amsterdam. He reinforces his vow of secrecy but promises to provide two allegorical riddles (enigmas) to aid worthy seekers in understanding the "Philosophical Nuptials."</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Physical Tincture, Amsterdam, secrecy, Pythagorean silence, Enigmas, Philosophical Nuptials, alchemical lineage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's warning and instruction to the "lover of the Art" (the alchemist), moving from the vow of secrecy to a metaphorical description of the purification process required for the "Philosophical Bride."</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

<header>✻ ✻ ✻ 56 ✻ ✻ ✻</header>

...[it] shall happen, unless it be done after the manner of the un-Christian Indians, who do not marry until the bride has been well washed in the river **Indus**, and has previously surrendered her virginity to another through cohabitation, and is impregnated thereby; when this has occurred, the bridegroom accepts his dear bride, who is thus accompanied by nine of her like, and knows her so that they become one. This occurs all the more surely because they are not only usually of one gender, but closely related and have remained well-befriended from youth.

If the lover of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, the secret science of transforming matter</note> has no river Indus in which to wash the **Philosophical Bride** <note>A symbolic term for the purified substance, often mercury or another metallic base, prepared for the alchemical "marriage"</note> in these parts, let him use in its place **Dragon’s Blood** <note>original: "Drachenbluth"; in alchemy, this usually refers to a red mineral like cinnabar or a specific red resin used as a reagent</note> sharpened with some salt; and if this also does not help, take some **Winged Eagle** <note>original: "geflügelten Adlers"; an alchemical code for a volatile substance that can be sublimated, such as sal ammoniac or mercury sublimate</note> crushed small, mixed with the same blood as much as may be enough. Rub her with it, or with fire, salt, water, or in **salt water** <note>original: "mari salso"; literally "in the salty sea," likely referring to a brine bath or a specific saline solution</note>, as the reader finds useful, though by a very warm fire. 

In this way, she will be cleansed of her coarse filth; let her sit for some time in a warm place to sweat through the help of her internal heat—indeed eight or nine times—until she becomes completely white and beautiful, like a **water-nymph**. Then she is sufficiently adorned to be **joined** <note>original: "copulirt"; to be united in the alchemical process of conjunction</note> with her bridegroom, and through the mixing of both their seeds, to bring a wonderful fruit into the world. Now, when the place of her resi-

...holds <note>Continuing from the previous page: "let he [be]holds..."</note>, because of the heat, initially tolerable, until the joined substance gradually **becomes accustomed** <note>original: "genet," likely an archaic form of "gewöhnet"; referring to the matter becoming stable and resistant to the external fire</note> to the external heat.

From this, such an excellent **subject** <note>original: "subjectum"; the material substance or "prime matter" undergoing transformation</note> is created, which, even if it were to fight against the mighty ruler on Earth **in the number ten** <note>original: "in numero denario"; in alchemy, the "number ten" refers to the process of "multiplication," where the power of the Stone is increased tenfold through successive distillations</note> for six hours, it would be recognized by everyone as having held the victory, and being an invincible **Monarch** of the world. 

Who then would not wish, who would not strive with all their strength, to obtain into his paternal power such a most noble, invincible fruit—indeed, such a **Fruit of Paradise** (which is by no means to be mistaken for the forbidden, evil apple) that is without any lack or flaw? Such a thing our highly enlightened **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss-German physician and alchemist famous for bridging the gap between chemistry and medicine</note>, mentioned at the beginning, undoubtedly possessed; he used it in a praiseworthy way for his neighbor's necessity and benefit, and also described it according to Philosophical custom. I do not doubt that all people who love Art and grace [would wish] for such a Fruit of Paradise from their hearts...

# THE FIRST RIDDLE <note>original: "ÆNIGMA PRIMVM"</note>

->1.<-

Found through fire is a stone
Which is common to the philosophers.

->2.<-

The same is truly not expensive,
Even if it possesses the fire of the wise.

->3.<-

Enclosed within itself with all diligence,
Since Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing; in alchemy, this refers to the heat of the furnace or the transformative power of fire.</note> made it very hot;

->4.<-

Then it is, as Basilius <note>Likely Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings were fundamental to the craft.</note> says,
Brought together from White and Red. <note>Refers to the *Albedo* (whiteness) and *Rubedo* (redness), the final stages of the Great Work representing silver and gold, or the feminine and masculine principles.</note>

->5.<-

It is a stone, and yet no stone,
But rather the first matter <note>original: "Materia Prima"; the formless, primal substance that alchemists believed was the starting point for all creation.</note>;

->6.<-

Which Lady Venus <note>In alchemical symbolism, Venus represents the metal copper and the "feminine" power of attraction and transformation.</note> gives pure and clean,
So that they both may be together.

->7.<-

So from it springs a clear little fountain,
Truly the second matter <note>original: "zweyt Materia"; often identified with "Philosophical Mercury," the fluid state of the matter after it has been purified.</note>.

->8.<-

Which is the mother of the Sun <note>The Sun (Sol) is the alchemical symbol for Gold and perfection.</note>,
Although her son is much older. <note>A common alchemical paradox implying that the eternal spirit of gold exists before its physical manifestation.</note>

<meta>catchword: 9. Now (original: "9. Nun")</meta>

<vocab>Riddle, Philosophers, Vulcan, Basilius, Matter, Venus, Sun, Little Fountain</vocab>

<summary>This page presents the first alchemical riddle, a poem describing the "Stone" as a substance born of fire, composed of "White and Red" principles, and originating from a "First Matter." It uses classical archetypes like Vulcan and Venus to mask the chemical and spiritual processes of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>First Riddle, Materia Prima, Vulcan, Basilius Valentinus, Venus, Alchemical Marriage, Sol, Alchemy poem</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Riddle" from page 59. The previous verses established the origin of the "first matter" and "second matter" (often identified as philosophical mercury). This section describes the final union or "alchemical wedding" that produces the Stone itself.</meta>

->9.<-

Now take her fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to high heat.</note> son entirely bare,
Lay him in Diana’s <note>The Roman goddess of the moon; in alchemical symbolism, Diana represents Silver or the "lunar" feminine principle.</note> lap.

->10.<-

Into her he shall pour his seed,
She shall enjoy him most excellently.

->11.<-

When they have fulfilled their love there,
That is the third matter <note>original: "dritt Materia"; the result of the union between the masculine (fixed) and feminine (volatile) principles, often called the "conjunction."</note>.

->12.<-

Which possesses such a power,
That she brings forth for you a beautiful son.

->13.<-

To this same child you should be very devoted,
For he makes you rich in gold,

->14.<-

Yes, he brings you to great honors,
And can bestow a long life upon you. <note>This refers to the dual nature of the Philosopher's Stone: the ability to transmute base metals into gold and the power to heal all diseases and extend life (the Elixir of Life).</note>

->15.<-

That is the end all at once;
If you understand it, then you are rich.

<summary>This page concludes the "First Riddle" by describing the sexualized allegory of the alchemical wedding, where the union of fixed and feminine principles produces the "Third Matter" and the final "son"—the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Diana, fixed son, third matter, alchemical wedding, gold, long life, philosopher's stone</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the conclusion of the First Riddle into the beginning of Riddle II. ## RIDDLE II. <note>original: "ÆNIGMA II."</note>

Six cities the King has despised,
And he regarded them as next to nothing;
The seventh he wished to inhabit,
For there a hall with red gold
Was hung, with ornament and decoration;
It was nobler than any from Ophir <note>Ophir was a biblical land famous for its high-quality gold; in alchemy, this refers to a gold more pure than the natural metal.</note>.
Believe freely what I report to you:
It was of the best in the world.
With this, Mars <note>The god of war, representing the metal Iron or a forceful, "martial" heat.</note> presented Apollo <note>The sun god, representing Gold or the solar principle.</note> as a gift,
And with it hung his hall,
When Mars plundered the kingdom of Venus <note>The goddess of love, representing the metal Copper.</note>,
And picked it out through Vulcan <note>The god of fire and the forge; in alchemy, Vulcan represents the secret fire used to extract the essence of metals.</note>;
Now hear what a strange thing
I will now bring to light:
The King, with mature counsel,
Has slept with his own mother,
Though she was already impregnated
By another man entirely.
There is more wonder at this time—
Yes, it is almost unbelievable—
That Apollo is truly older
Than his dear, pure mother.

<vocab>Riddle, Mars, Apollo, Venus, Vulcan, Ophir, Enigma, Mythology</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the Second Riddle, using a complex mythological allegory involving the planetary gods (Mars, Venus, Apollo, and Vulcan) to describe the refinement of the "King" and the paradoxical, circular nature of alchemical generation.</summary>
<keywords>Riddle II, planetary metals, red gold, Mars, Apollo, Venus, Vulcan, Ophir, philosophical incest, alchemical genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page concludes "Riddle II," which began on the previous page. The text finishes the description of the "King" in his golden hall and transitions from the mythological allegory to a direct address to the reader, emphasizing the spiritual and practical requirements of the alchemical "Work."</meta>

Already dwelling in his hall,
And there the Sun and Moon <note>In alchemy, the Sun and Moon represent the celestial counterparts to Gold and Silver, or the active (Sulfur) and passive (Mercury) principles of nature.</note> govern,
He can make everything within his realm
Equal to his own gold;
My dear reader, whoever you may be,
If you now understand what this is,
And do not miss the mark by a single hair,
Then you are truly wise and wealthy;
Henceforth, pray to God for the right understanding,
Be secretive <note>original: "verschwiegen"; this refers to the Hermetic vow of silence, where practitioners were sworn to keep the secrets of the art hidden from the uninitiated.</note>, and take the work <note>The "Work" (Latin: *Opus*) refers to the practical laboratory process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in hand.

🜛 <term>Phlegm</term> <note>original: "Phlegmata." In an alchemical context, this refers to the watery, tasteless, and odorless liquid that is either the first or last part to come over during distillation.</note>

♄ <term>Lead</term> <note>original: "Plumbum" (Latin) and "Bley" (German). Historically associated with the planet Saturn.</note>

🜖 <term>To Precipitate</term> <note>original: "Præcipitare" (Latin) and "Niederſchlagen" (German). The process of causing a solid substance to separate and settle at the bottom of a liquid solution.</note>

p <term>Pinch</term> <note>original: "Pugillus" (Latin). A measurement defined as "as much as one can grab with five fingers."</note>

🜝 <term>Powder</term> <note>original: "Pulvis" (Latin) and "Pulver" (German).</note>

q.s <term>Sufficient Quantity</term> <note>original: "Quantum Satis" (Latin). A common instruction meaning "as much as is enough" to achieve the desired result.</note>

Q.E <term>Quintessence</term> <note>original: "Quint Essentia" (Latin). The "fifth essence" or the purest, most concentrated medicinal or spiritual force within a substance.</note>

R or ℞ <term>Take</term> <note>original: "Recipere" (Latin) and "Nim" (German). The traditional opening command for a recipe or prescription.</note>

🜳 <term>Regulus</term> <note>original: "Regulus." The metallic part of a mineral that sinks to the bottom of the crucible during smelting; it refers particularly to the metallic form of Antimony.</note>

<summary>This page functions as a reference glossary for alchemical and pharmaceutical symbols, defining terms for specific substances, quantities like a "pinch," and procedural instructions like "mix" or "precipitate."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy symbols, Mercury Precipitate, Saltpeter, Lead, Regulus, Phlegm, laboratory shorthand, early modern chemistry</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical reference guide to alchemical symbols and laboratory shorthand, continuing from the previous entries which ended with Quintessence. This section covers letters R through T, detailing equipment, salts, measurements, and various chemical principles.</meta>

q <term>Retort</term> <note>original: "Retorta" (Latin) and "Thonb Schnabel" (German, literally 'clay beak'). A laboratory vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note>

θ <term>Salt</term> <note>original: "Sal" (Latin) and "Saltz" (German).</note>

✳ <term>Salt of Antimony</term> <note>original: "Sal Antimonium." A salt derived from antimony, often used in medicinal preparations.</note>

8 <term>Rock Salt</term> <note>original: "Sal gemma" (Latin) and "Stein Saltz" (German). Naturally occurring common salt, or halite.</note>

θv <term>Volatile Salt</term> <note>original: "Sal Volatile" (Latin) and "flüchtiges Saltz" (German). Usually refers to ammonium carbonate, known for its ability to vaporize easily.</note>

Э <term>Scruple</term> <note>original: "Scrupulus" (Latin). An apothecary unit of weight. The text defines it as the "3rd part of a dram (Quentlein) or 20 grains."</note>

sa <term>According to the Art</term> <note>original: "Secundum Artem" (Latin) and "nach der Kunst" (German). A directive to perform a task with professional skill and following established alchemical procedures.</note>

ß <term>Half</term> <note>original: "Semissis" or "semis" (Latin) and "der halbe Theil" (German). Used in recipes to indicate half of a given weight or volume.</note>

♎ <term>Spirit</term> <note>original: "Spiritus" (Latin) and "Geist" (German). In the alchemical sense, this refers to the volatile essence of a substance that can be captured through distillation.</note>

V/ <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Vini" (Latin). High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>

♃ <term>Tin</term> <note>original: "Stannum" (Latin) and "Zin" (German). This metal is traditionally associated with the planet Jupiter.</note>

sss <term>Layer upon Layer</term> <note>original: "Stratum super Stratum" (Latin) and "schicht auff schicht" (German). A method of filling a vessel by alternating layers of different materials, often for calcination or infusion.</note>

♎ <term>To Sublimate</term> <note>original: "Sublimare" (Latin) and "erhitzen" (German, literally 'to heat'). The process of heating a substance so it turns directly from a solid to a vapor and then back to a solid, used to purify materials.</note>

⏃ <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur" (Latin) and "Schwefel" (German). One of the three "prime" principles of alchemy (the Tria Prima), representing combustibility.</note>

X <term>Talc</term> <note>original: "Talcum" (Latin) and "Talck" (German). Described here as "greenish-shining, and a stone that feels greasy to the touch."</note>

⊞ <term>Tartar</term> <note>original: "Tartarus" (Latin) and "weinstein" (German). A deposit of potassium bitartrate that forms in wine barrels, used frequently in alchemical experiments.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a reference glossary for alchemical and apothecary symbols, focusing on salts, laboratory processes like sublimation, and units of measurement such as the scruple.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical symbols, retort, salt, volatile salt, scruple, spirit of wine, tin, sulfur, talc, tartar, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 280 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical reference guide to alchemical symbols and laboratory shorthand. The previous page concluded with "Stannum" (Tin) and "Spiritus" (Spirit). This section moves into entries beginning with T, U, V, and Z, covering elemental earth, various chemical salts, glass, and units of time and weight.</meta>

🜃 **Earth** <note>original: "Terra" (Latin) and "Erde" (German). One of the four classical elements, representing the solid, cold, and dry state of matter.</note>

℞ **Tincture** <note>original: "Tinctura" (Latin) and "Tinctur" (German). A solution, usually alcoholic, containing the "essence" of a medicinal or chemical substance.</note>

**Tutty** <note>original: "Tutia" (Latin). Described in the German text as "a substance that attaches itself to wood placed over metals during smelting." This refers to crude zinc oxide that collected in the chimneys of smelting furnaces.</note>

V. **Wine** <note>original: "Vinum" (Latin) and "Wein" (German). Frequently used as a solvent in alchemical preparations.</note>

⊕ **Verdigris** <note>original: "Viride æris" (Latin) and "grün Span" (German). Literally "green of copper," this is the green pigment or crust that forms on copper, brass, or bronze.</note>

⊕ **Vitriol** <note>original: "Vitriolum" (Latin). A general term for sulfate minerals, such as iron sulfate (green vitriol) or copper sulfate (blue vitriol), which were essential for producing acids.</note>

XX or XX **Glass** <note>original: "Vitrum" (Latin) and "Glas" (German).</note>

℥ **Ounce** <note>original: "Untia" (Latin) and "eine Untze." A unit of weight. The text specifies it is equal to "2 <term>Loth</term>" (a traditional German weight equivalent to about 1/32 of a pound).</note>

℥ß **Half-ounce** <note>original: "Untia semissis" (Latin) and "halbe Untze" (German).</note>

⊡ **Urine** <note>original: "Urina" (Latin) and "Urin" (German). Used in various processes as a source of phosphorus and ammonia.</note>

✳ **Sal Ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal Armoniacum" (Latin). Ammonium chloride, a pungent white crystalline salt used as a flux in metallurgy and in various medicinal recipes.</note>

♋ **Cancer** <note>original: "Cancer" (Latin). In alchemy, this refers both to the astrological sign (the Crab) and the chemical process of calcination or the month associated with it.</note>

**Ashes** <note>original: "Cineres" (Latin) and "Asche" (German). Refers to the remains of burnt organic or mineral matter, often used for lye or as a filtering agent.</note>

33 **Cinnabar** <note>original: "Zinnober" (German). A bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, the primary ore from which mercury is extracted.</note>

⧖ **Hour** <note>original: "Hora" (Latin). A unit of time for heating or digestion processes.</note>

**Borax** <note>original: "Borras" (German). Described here as "a flux powder" (ein flus Pulver). It was used to lower the melting point of minerals and to clean metal surfaces.</note>

**Bismuth** <note>original: "Bismuthum" (Latin). A brittle, silvery-white metal that was often confused with lead or tin in early chemistry.</note>

<summary>This page concludes an alphabetical glossary of alchemical symbols, focusing on substances like Earth, Tutty, and Cinnabar, as well as measurements like the Ounce and the Hour. It serves as a practical decoding key for interpreting early modern chemical recipes and laboratory notes.</summary>

<keywords>Earth, Tutty, Vitriol, Sal Ammoniac, Cinnabar, Borax, Bismuth, alchemical symbols, apothecary weights, smelting byproducts</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the reference guide to alchemical and medical shorthand. While the previous page focused on chemical elements and substances like earth, glass, and vitriol, this page transitions into the specific units of measurement used by apothecaries and physicians to prepare medicines.</meta>

## Medicinal weights

℔ 1 One Pound <note>original: "℔ j ein Pfund." The symbol ℔ represents the Latin "libra."</note>

℔ ss Half a Pound <note>original: "℔ ss ein Halb Pfund." The "ss" is an abbreviation for the Latin "semis," meaning half.</note>

℥ 1 One Ounce <note>original: "℥ j ein Unze."</note>

℥ 2 Two Ounces

℥ ss Half an ounce

ℨ 1 One <term>Quentchen</term> or Drachma, which is 3 Scruples. <note>original: "ℨ j ein Quenty oder Drachma." A Quentchen was a traditional German weight; in the apothecary system, it was equivalent to a drachm.</note>

ℨ ss Half a <term>Quentchen</term>

℈ 1 One Scruple, which has 20 grains. <note>original: "℈ j ein Scrupel hat 20 grän." This follows the standard apothecary system where 20 grains make one scruple.</note>

℈ 2 Two Scruples

Gr. 1 One Grain <note>original: "Grj ein Gran hat 3 Grän." This likely refers to the grain as the smallest unit of measurement.</note>

Gutt. 1 One Little Drop <note>original: "Guttj ein Tröpflein," from the Latin "gutta" meaning drop.</note>

M. <term>Manipulus</term>, one handful. <note>A measurement used for herbs, referring to the amount of material that can be grasped in one hand.</note>

aa Of each, as much as the other. <note>original: "aa eines so viel als das andere." Derived from the Greek "ana," this shorthand tells the pharmacist to use equal parts of all the listed ingredients.</note>

℞ Take. <note>original: "R. Recipe Nim." The "℞" symbol is an abbreviation for the Latin "recipe" (the imperative form of "to take"). It remains the standard symbol for medical prescriptions today.</note>

<summary>This page defines the shorthand symbols and units of measurement used in early modern pharmacy, ranging from pounds and ounces to handfuls and individual drops.</summary>
<keywords>apothecary weights, measurements, pound, ounce, scruple, grain, drachma, prescription symbols</keywords>
